Paalberg prolonge

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Arrivé chez les Verts au dernier mercato d'hiver, Marten-Chris Paalberg, 18 ans seulement et international A à moins de 17 ans avec sa sélection Estonienne s'inscrit dans la durée avec les Verts.

Le club vient de communiquer sur la prolongation de l'attaquant jusqu'en 2030.

Une belle marque de confiance pour un joueur qui, aura d'abord du se remettre d'une blessure avant de pouvoir porter la tunique verte avec la réserve depuis seulement quelques semaines.

Rappelé en sélection pendant cette trêve internationale, celui qui a débuté dans l'élite Estonienne à seulement 15 ans devra patienter encore un peu avant de débuter sa carrière avec les pros et participer activement au développement de l'équipe comme il l'exprime sur le site officiel : "Je me sens bien intégré, sur le terrain comme en dehors, et je suis pleinement déterminé à poursuivre ma progression pour contribuer aux ambitions du club sur le long terme. J'ai hâte de poursuivre ce chapitre."

Pas tendre Zoumana !

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Allez Paillade, le site non officiel dédié à l'actualité du Montpellier HSC revient sur la surprenante défaite à domicile des Héraultais contre le FC Metz (2-3) et relate les propos du coach Zoumana Camara, pas tendre avec ses joueurs qui ont laissé filer une occasion de rester dans la roue des Verts :

"Je suis déçu. La leçon, c’est qu’un match se joue 90 minutes. On a été absent des débats sur les 45 premières minutes. Et même sur cette deuxième mi-temps où on est revenu avec de meilleures intentions. Quand on met ce 2-1, on ne doit pas encaisser ce troisième but. On a le sentiment d’avoir fait le plus dur, de pouvoir emballer le match et de créer le doute. Et derrière, on prend un troisième but quelques minutes après. Le sentiment que rien ne tourne dans le bon sens dans cette soirée.

L’absence d’agressivité, dans l’intensité, dans le rythme. Quand on ne met pas ces ingrédients-là, on tombe face à une équipe où il y avait un tout. Une équipe qui a pris un sévère revers à la maison, qui vient de perdre dans la semaine son président historique. Et puis un ancien joueur de la maison qui revient. Donc vous mettez tout ça ensemble, ça crée un esprit et une équipe qui est venue en mode commando, en mode combat avec l’envie de faire quelque chose. Et si nous, on n’a pas la capacité à avoir au moins cette même envie, cette même agressivité, cette même détermination avant de parler du jeu, on ne peut pas exister dans ce championnat et on n’a pas existé aujourd’hui, en tout cas sur la première période.

(Un relâchement ?) Peut-être. J’ai des jeunes joueurs, on est en train d’apprendre un peu dans tout. C’est l’avenir qui dira si c’est un accident ou pas. Mais en tout cas, on était absent des débats aujourd’hui, surtout sur la première mi-temps.

(Un coup d’arrêt ?) Une saison, c’est long. C’est très très long. L’environnement était euphorique. Et parfois ultra-enthousiaste. J’ai dû la saison passée venir beaucoup de fois ici. On parlait d’être un peu plus réaliste face à la situation. Aujourd’hui, les résultats faisaient qu’il y avait cette euphorie. Et c’est juste à juste titre. C’est à nous de devoir, à l’intérieur, canaliser ça, garder les pieds sur terre, continuer à avancer et continuer à mettre les ingrédients qui ont fait la réussite. Et là, on ne les a pas mis ce soir."

Stassin, le derby pour convaincre les sceptiques ?

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Arrivé tout début septembre à Séville en prêt avec option d’achat, Lucas Stassin présente après 7 matchs disputés un bilan mitigé. S’il a la confiance de son coach, ses stats restent maigres (1 but et 1 passe décisive).

Les médias espagnols, circonspects, considèrent que les prochains matchs, en particulier le derby contre le Betis prévu juste après la trêve internationale (le 21 novembre) seront déterminants pour l’attaquant belge :

Le site internet dédié au FC Sévile Vamos mi Sevilla fait part de ses premiers doutes :

L'arrivée de Lucas Stassin a été l'un des transferts les plus marquants du FC Séville lors du dernier mercato estival. L'attaquant belge est arrivé à Nervión en prêt de Saint-Étienne avec pour mission de dynamiser l'attaque de l'équipe entraînée par Luis García Plaza. Cependant, au fil des semaines, les attentes placées dans le jeune attaquant se sont estompées, puisqu'il traverse actuellement une période de disette. Depuis son but en septembre face à l’Espanyol Barcelone, l'attaquant belge n'a pas trouvé le chemin des filets et peine à se montrer suffisamment présent dans le dernier tiers du terrain pour menacer régulièrement les défenses adverses. Tous les regards sont tournés vers le derby, une occasion de rédemption. Pour Stassin, la rencontre face aux éternels rivaux représente l'occasion idéale de dissiper les doutes apparus en début de saison, de mettre fin à la série de défaites et de redonner espoir à des supporters qui attendent avec impatience la victoire.

Pour le Diario de Sevilla, au-delà de Stassin, les difficultés concernent l’ensemble du secteur offensif :

Bien que le potentiel offensif des nouveaux attaquants de Séville, Lucas Stassin et Robbie Uré, reste à confirmer, les principaux problèmes de l'équipe lors des derniers matchs ont été la création d'occasions de but. Séville doit retrouver sa fluidité de jeu, trouver de meilleures lignes de passe et veiller à ce que ses attaquants reçoivent le ballon dans des conditions plus favorables.

Enfin, selon AS, les attaquants étaient les recrues les plus excitantes de l'été, mais leurs performances à Nervión n'ont pas encore décollé. Stassin a été gêné par quelques problèmes physiques qui, sans être graves, l'ont empêché d'atteindre son meilleur niveau (…) Pour l'instant, la patience est de mise à Séville, mais le temps presse.

Espérons que Lucas profite de sa convocation avec l’équipe Belge Espoirs pour retrouver le chemin des filets, avant, qui sait, d’être de nouveau le héros d’un derby.

On-Device Speech Recognition and Text-to-Speech in Google AI Edge Gallery

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Today, we are introducing on-device Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) and Text-to-Speech (TTS) capabilities in Google AI Edge Gallery. Voice is often the most natural way to interact with AI on phones and wearables. Recent advances in compact speech architectures now make it possible to run accurate, multilingual ASR and expressive TTS models entirely on device, all within a small memory footprint, with low latency, and completely offline. By pairing these lightweight speech models with on-device small language models (SLMs) like Gemini Nano and Gemma 4, developers can build apps that stream live transcriptions as the user speaks and synthesize natural spoken replies locally.

This release brings together major advancements across both Google’s system speech models and the open-weight ecosystem:

Below, we’ll explore how this local voice pipeline powers the new XR (Extended Reality) Language Teacher showcase, upgrades AI Chat in Google AI Edge Gallery, and how you can build with these APIs and models today.

Learning a language while traveling happens in the real world, often in underground stations, busy markets, or abroad where Wi-Fi and roaming data may not be available. To show how vision, language, and speech models can work together 100% locally on your phone with no network connection needed, we built the XR Language Teacher showcase in Google AI Edge Gallery. Simply point your camera at an unfamiliar object or menu, ask "What is that, and how do I ask for one?", and immediately hear an explanation in your language alongside authentic pronunciation in the local language.

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

With inference running locally on a paired phone, the same experience works standalone on a phone and extends naturally to hands-free smart glasses. To see how we adapted Language Teacher for intelligent glasses check out our recent talk at Android Dev Summit: Building Audio-First Experiences for Intelligent Eyewear with Gemini Nano.

Beyond the XR Language Teacher showcase, we also built ASR and TTS directly into Google AI Edge Gallery’s main AI Chat feature, giving on-device SLMs like Gemini Nano and Gemma 4 a natural, offline voice interface while on the go.

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

While multimodal models like Gemma 4 can process audio inputs directly, we provide a modular solution for cascading language models with separate, lightweight voice models for latency optimization and flexibility. Depending on your target devices and model requirements, our open-source code on GitHub shows you the optimized and recommended solution for Android using ML Kit, as well as MediaPipe Tasks and LiteRT for cross-platform coverage and open model support.

For developers building for Android devices, ML Kit’s GenAI Speech Recognition and Text-to-Speech APIs provide a standardized, platform-integrated path out of the box. With the underlying speech models managed through AICore and Google’s on-device speech services, developers get production-grade quality without having to manage model downloads or worry about APK size bloat. Your app also benefits automatically from hardware acceleration across NPU and TPU backends (when available) and seamless background model updates.

Integrating ASR and TTS into cross-platform applications usually requires handling low-level audio framing, Voice Activity Detection (VAD), and complex decoding pipelines. MediaPipe Task simplifies this by providing unified, turnkey ASR and TTS APIs across iOS, macOS, Windows, Linux, and Web that abstract away manual pre-processing, audio stream routing, and platform-specific audio management into simple, high-level interfaces:

Under the hood, ML Kit and MediaPipe Tasks are powered by LiteRT, Google’s high-performance on-device AI inference infrastructure. Cross-platform applications requiring even more fine-grained control over speech processing or custom model architectures can also build on it directly.

Speech models often rely on multiple subgraphs and auxiliary assets, such as encoder/decoder subgraphs and tokenizers for ASR, or phonemizer dictionaries, voice embeddings, and vocoders for TTS. All required components for each model are packaged into a single .litertlm file for easy deployment. The app ships with ready-to-use .litertlm models, including Parakeet-TDT-0.6B-v3 (multilingual ASR), PocketTTS 100M (multilingual TTS), Phonon 31M (ultra-lightweight English TTS), and Kokoro-82M (multilingual TTS). Beyond these, the vibrant Hugging Face LiteRT Community with over 10K+ followers offers a growing catalog of open-weight models, like Whisper, Qwen3-ASR and Moonshine for ASR, or Qwen3-TTS and Matcha for TTS. For developers seeking custom implementations or support for additional open-weight voice models, LiteRT provides a rich set of multimodal model examples to help you get started, with more on the way.

Adding a local voice pipeline takes on-device SLMs beyond typing-based chat interfaces, enabling natural, voice-first interactions that work anywhere, completely offline. All the building blocks to try these experiences or integrate them into your own apps are available today:

We'd like to extend a special thanks to our significant contributors for their work on this project:

Alice Zheng, Ajay Dudani, Alex Kanaukou, Amy Zeppenfeld, Byungchul Kim, Cheng Yang, Chintan Parikh, Chris Parsons, Cormac Brick, David Chou, Dong Chen, Geonsun Lee, Glenn Cameron, Hriday Chhabria, Ian Ballantyne, Ireneu Pla, James O’Donnell, Jamieson Pryor, Jason Poon, Jenn Lee, Jianing Wei, Jing Jin, Jingxiao Zheng, Jingyu Shi, Karthik Thirumalai, Kristen Wright, Lu Wang, Marius Kintel, Matthias Grundmann, Miao Wang, Mogan Shieh, Na Li, Paul Hsia, Qiumin Xu, Ravi Shankar, Rishika Sinha, Ronald Wotzlaw, Sachin Kotwani, Sandeep Patil, Sebastian Schmidt, Shao-Fu Shih, Shuangfeng Li, Suril Shah, Tiger Souvannakoumane, Tzu-Ping Chen, Weiyi Wang, Wentao Tu, Yang Yang, Yi-Chun Kuo, Yishuang Pang, Yu-che Liu, Yu-hui Chen, Yuling Liang, Zhihao Yang, Zichuan Wei.

What is Pathways? Single-controller training for large models

Anthropic takes on Google and Microsoft with Claude Sheets

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The company also has a new whiteboard app.

Anthropic is significantly expanding Claude's suite of productivity tools. At an event in San Francisco today, the company announced Claude Sheets. As you can probably guess, Sheets allows Claude to generate and edit spreadsheets for you. Each file can include its own set of tabs and formulas. You'll also find the usual assortment of formatting options.

Alongside Claude Sheets, Anthropic announced two other new products: Claude Meetings and Claude Whiteboards. The first of those is Anthropic's take on a meeting agent like the kind offered by Otter.ai and other companies. Available in Google Meet to start (which, incidentally, offers its own meeting agent through Gemini), Claude can join your video calls and start taking notes as you and your colleagues talk. You can later chat with Claude about any plans or action items that came up during your call. "Claude marks itself as a participant and posts a notice in the meeting's chat," says Anthropic. "It won't join a call with outside guests unless an admin allows it, and it leaves if one joins anyway." At the moment, Claude Meetings is only available to Team and Enterprise customers.

Moving to Claude Whiteboards, it's a pretty cutesy take on a collaborative brainstorming app. As your team works together, Claude will group everyone's ideas together by theme and draft action items for each individual. In a nifty bit of integration, you can use Claude Meetings and Whiteboards at the same time. Anthropic is billing the tool as a natural extension of how people already use Claude. "Many people already brainstorm with Claude, but sharing the results with coworkers has meant copy-pasting them somewhere else," the company says. Claude Whiteboards is available in beta to all paid users across the web, desktop and mobile.

Separately from those announcements, Anthropic is renaming Claude Dashboards to Claude Data and starting to roll out the tool to all paid users. Anthropic says it decided to rename the app because "companies use their data for more than dashboards." In any case, you can connect Claude Data to your company's CRM so that the chatbot can then answer specific questions you might have about what's inside.

Lastly, Anthropic is upgrading Claude's plugin functionality, which allows you to connect apps like Canva and Notion to the chatbot so it can pull information from those services. As Anthropic notes, there are already more than 9,000 connectors offered through the Claude Marketplace, but now you can open full apps inside of Claude, allowing the chatbot to act on what it sees in the window. Anthropic is rolling out this update starting with a small group of partners that includes Adobe, Atlassian and HubSpot.

Google Fi announces super network automatic carrier switching for improved speed and reception

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

It’s rolling out first on Pixel phones before wider availability arrives in 2027.

Getting the fastest mobile data speeds can be tricky because a single carrier may not have optimal coverage in your area. So to address this, Google Fi is introducing its new super network feature which allows your phone to automatically switch between two different carriers to deliver the best connection possible.

The super network feature begins rolling out today to customers with Pixel phones (Pixel 9 and above with eSIMs). In the event that your phone detects that your connection is sluggish, it will automatically switch from one carrier to another. Unfortunately, Google Fi is not officially disclosing the names of the carriers it's using for its super network, though based on previous partnerships one of them is probably T-Mobile. Regardless, the effect is that if you hit a dead zone or congestion, your phone can connect to a different cell signal without any manual input.

However, that's not all, because alongside the super network, Google Fi is also introducing a new Wi-Fi Auto-Connect+ feature designed to improve data speeds indoors in places like malls, airports and more. That means even if you're in a building that interferes with traditional cellular reception, your phone can switch over to Wi-Fi where available, while automatic encryption ensures that your data stays secure. Wi-Fi Auto-Connect+ will initially be available on Pixel phones (Pixel 5a and newer) and some Motorola and Samsung phones (running OneUI 9 and above) across select locations throughout the US, Europe, Asia and Brazil.

But perhaps the best part is that both Google Fi's super network and Wi-Fi Auto Connect+ will be available to customers with Unlimited Premium and Flexible plans at no extra cost. Furthermore, for international travelers, both features will work in over 200 destinations across the globe.

Alternatively, for customers who need a little extra help or technical support, the company is rolling out its new AI-powered Ask Fi agent inside the app. It's designed to answer questions, straighten out billing concerns or help troubleshoot things like connectivity issues in real time. Thankfully, Google Fi says there will still be actual humans you can reach out to if that's what you prefer.

DHT Holdings (DHT) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Record tanker rates drove results and a 67th straight dividend payout.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

DHT Holdings, Inc. (DHT +1.22%) reported record financial results for the third quarter of 2026, surpassing the previous record set in the second quarter. Management reported that the performance was driven by a supportive tanker market and strong commercial execution across its fleet. The company maintained its capital allocation policy of returning 100% of ordinary net income to shareholders while expanding its fleet through a newbuilding program and strategic vessel sales. The company stated that ongoing logistical disruptions and shifting crude procurement patterns have increased transportation distances, sustaining elevated freight rates.

Operator: Good day, and thank you for standing by. Welcome to the Q3 2026 DHT Holdings, Inc. Earnings Conference Call. [Operator Instructions] Please be advised that today's conference is being recorded. I would now like to hand the conference over to your first speaker today, Laila Halvorsen, CFO. Please go ahead.

Laila Halvorsen: Thank you. Good morning and good afternoon, everyone. Welcome, and thank you for joining DHT Holdings Third Quarter 2026 Earnings Call. I'm joined by DHT's President and CEO, Svein Moxnes Harfjeld. As usual, we will go through financials and some highlights before we open up for your questions. The link to the slide deck can be found on our website, dhtankers.com. Before we get started with today's call, I would like to make the following remarks. A replay of this conference call will be available on our website, dhtankers.com, until November 17. In addition, our earnings press release will be available on our website and on the SEC EDGAR system as an exhibit to our Form 6-K.

As a reminder, on this conference call, we will discuss matters that are forward-looking in nature. These forward-looking statements are based on our current expectations about future events as detailed in our financial report. Actual results may differ materially from the expectations reflected in these forward-looking statements. We urge you to read our periodic reports available on our website and on the SEC EDGAR system, including the risk factors in these reports for more information regarding risks that we face. As usual, we'll start the presentation with some financial highlights. The third quarter of 2026 was a record quarter for the company, surpassing the previous record achieved in the second quarter of 2026.

The results reflected a supportive tanker market and strong commercial performance. In the third quarter, we achieved revenues on TCE basis of $290 million and adjusted EBITDA of $269.5 million. Net income came in at $270.9 million, equal to $1.68 per share. After adjusting for the noncash fair value gain related to interest rate derivatives of $2.2 million and the gain on sale of DHT Bauhinia of $34.2 million, we had ordinary net income for the quarter of $234.5 million, equal to $1.45 per share. Vessel operating expenses for the quarter were $19 million and G&A for the quarter was $4.3 million.

In terms of market performance, our vessels trading in the spot market earned an average of $167,100 per day, while the vessels on time charters achieved $114,200 per day. The average combined TCE for the fleet in the quarter was $141,300 per day. We continue to maintain a very strong balance sheet, supported by conservative leverage and robust liquidity. At the end of the third quarter, total liquidity was $555 million, consisting of $154.7 million in cash and $400.7 million available under our revolving credit facilities. At quarter end, financial leverage was 10.5% based on market values for the fleet and net debt was about $15 million per vessel, well below estimated residual values.

Looking at our cash flow, we began the quarter with a cash balance of $162 million. During the quarter, operations generated $269.5 million in EBITDA. Debt repayment and cash interest totaled $17 million. Proceeds from sale of vessel was $51 million and $197 million was distributed to shareholders through a cash dividend. In addition, we invested $4.6 million in vessels and $91 million in vessel under construction. Changes in working capital and other items amounted to $93 million, and the quarter ended with $154.7 million in cash.

In line with our capital allocation policy of paying out 100% of ordinary net income as quarterly cash dividends, the Board has approved a dividend of $1.45 per share for the third quarter of '26. This marks our 67th consecutive quarterly cash dividend. The shares will trade ex-dividend on November 23, and the dividend will be paid on November 30 to shareholders of record as of November 23. On the left side of this slide, we also present our estimated P&L and cash breakeven level for next year.

Our P&L breakeven for 2027 is currently estimated at $29,200 per day, while our cash breakeven is currently estimated at $20,600 per day, which reflects all true cash costs, meaning OpEx, G&A, CapEx, debt installments and cash interest. The difference between our P&L and cash breakeven is currently estimated at an average of $8,600 per day per vessel. The discretionary cash flow will remain within the company and be allocated for general corporate purposes. On this slide, we present an update on bookings to date for the fourth quarter of 2026. We expect 1,174 time-charter days covered for the fourth quarter at an average rate of $100,200 per day.

This rate includes profit sharing for the month of October and the base rate only for November and December for contracts with profit sharing features. Two of our vessels operate under time-charter arrangements with profit sharing mechanism, allowing them to participate in the spot market upside. We also anticipate 930 spot days for the quarter, of which 68% or 630 days have been booked at an average rate of $579,900 per day. These bookings reflect a very strong market environment and key up for another quarter with record earnings.

Importantly, none of the booked spot days relate to voyages in the Persian Gulf, demonstrating that earnings have been achieved across our broader trading portfolio and in line with DHT's risk appetite. The first 9 months of 2026 have already exceeded the company's previous record full year financial performance by a good margin. Given the strength of bookings to date with the fourth quarter expecting to surpass the third quarter, 2026 will establish a new annual earnings record for the company. The spot P&L breakeven for the quarter is estimated to be below 0 as earnings from our time-charter portfolio are expected to more than cover forecasted operating, financing and overhead costs.

And now I'll turn the call over to Svein.

Svein Moxnes Harfjeld: Thank you, Laila. I will now briefly walk through our key quarterly highlights. We delivered the DHT Bauhinia, built 2007, to the new owners in July 2026. Cash proceeds from the sale were $51 million, whilst the capital gain was $34.2 million. We took delivery of a newbuilding, the DHT Impala on July 24. This was some 5 months earlier than the contractual commitment by the yard and marks the fourth and final vessel in our 2026 newbuilding program. The vessel entered the spot market with a highly rewarding start to her life with us. We entered into 3-year time-charter contracts for DHT Jaguar, built 2015, and DHT Panther, built 2016, with rates at $75,000 and $100,000 per day, respectively.

The vessels delivered into their services in September and October. We have completed dry dockings for 7 vessels year-to-date. All projects were completed on schedule and within budget, marking the completion of our 2026 drydocking program. We have 4 vessels scheduled for dry docking during 2027. These are DHT Sundarbans, DHT Opal and DHT Taiga, all built in 2012, which will undergo their third special surveys, whereas DHT Tiger, built in 2017, will undergo her second special survey. And now moving to events subsequent to the quarter. In line with our long-term fleet development plans, we have entered into an additional newbuilding contract with Hanwha Ocean.

This will be the sixth addition to the Antelope class, which commenced delivery in January '26. She will be named DHT Sable and will also feature large carrying capacity and come equipped with an exhaust gas cleaning system. She is scheduled for delivery in April '29 and leaves us with an order book of 2 VLCC buildings -- newbuildings, the first scheduled for delivery in August '28. Both orders have been concluded with what should be assumed to be early and competitive delivery slots. These orders will be financed by internal resources, cash flow from operations and projected new mortgage debt. We will share some thoughts on the order book and what it potentially could mean for DHT.

The current order book now stands at 320 ships over the coming 4 years against the fleet of 940 ships. The fleet total includes ships storing oil. We expect 44% of the fleet to be older than 15 years by the end of 2030, 30% older than 20 years and 14% older than 25, hence the global fleet renewal is required. These numbers assume no scrapping. China is developing its shipyard industry at an impressive pace and is set to construct some 77% of the current VLCC order book. Within the Chinese shipyards representing this part of the order book, some have experience in building large tankers and some have ambitious plans for this space.

Looking under the hood of the total order book, we have at our liberty broken it down to 4 categories being; 1, traditional shipowners; 2, oil companies/traders; 3, national companies; and 4, financial actors. We believe parts of the order book to represent ships or fleets that not necessarily have natural homes. We have built up a strong and resourceful balance sheet to support continued investments in our fleet and customer offerings when the right opportunities appear. We think it's reasonable to expect that some of such opportunities could evolve out of the order book in due course, hence the perceived threat could become an opportunity to DHT.

The freight market remains strong due to ongoing logistical disruptions resulting in operational inefficiencies in combination with shifting crude procurement patterns expanding transportation distances. Geopolitical risk persists because recent events demonstrate for the first time in history that the full or partial closure of the Strait of Hormuz is possible, altering long-term risk perceptions even if regular traffic resumes. Supply chain resilience is a priority for Middle Eastern oil producers, but expanding their logistical capabilities will not automatically restore the tanker fleet's historical productivity levels. Energy security concerns should drive a major inventory restocking cycle by governments and corporations, which is expected to boost transportation demand above actual consumption levels.

Private consolidation is tightening market dynamics following a structural fleet consolidation by a private aggregator earlier in the year, resulting in fragmented spot supply and restricted information flows. Limited effective fleet supply growth due to an aging fleet, a growing share of vessels operating outside mainstream trades and a limited near-term newbuilding delivery schedule. News flows and U.S. midterm election results move commodity and capital markets and fuels volatility, including significant near-term deltas between physical and derivative markets for freight as well as creating large bid-ask spreads in term contracts. And with that, operator, we are now ready to open the floor for questions.

Operator: [Operator Instructions] And our first question comes from the line of Omar Nokta from Clarksons.

Omar Nokta: Obviously, very strong update, very good guidance and the market continues to be very fluid and fast changing. And maybe just on your last couple of points, I wanted to just follow up. Clearly, spot rates have been volatile. They've been elevated, but they have come down here over the past month or so from their very elevated levels back in early September, still strong.

But just as you think about where things are going, if we get to a point where the U.S. and Iran have reached an agreement that there seems to be at least expectations given the shift in the Brent curve, how do you think this VLCC market looks as we move forward in a scenario where you have a reopening of "Hormuz" versus what we're seeing today via the shuttling and ship-to-ship transfer market?

Svein Moxnes Harfjeld: Omar, I think the news flow proven this year has been very, very volatile, right? And we had news in one direction one day and news in another direction in the next day and some of the psychology spills into, of course, markets and how they're playing out. I think it's actually very hard to know how this will work now and whether there is a deal or not, right? And -- but I really think that if for sake of argument, there is a deal with some level of opening or resumption of traffic, it will take quite a while to unwind all these inefficiencies that is in place, right?

And -- so I have a hard time to see that anything falling out of bed, frankly. We have ships in the market all the time. And as of present, there is still significant interest at very good levels for vessels that we have available in the spot market. So -- and it will vary a bit from trade to trade. I would say that trades in and around the Middle East are probably holding up a bit better than some of the South American trades. But the market is dynamic. And if there are big differences, one basin will pull ships from another basin and so forth. So I don't think there's any reason here to be overly concerned.

But you can say in nominal terms, if the market drops from, say, $800,000, $900,000 a day to $600,000 a day, it's a significant change that is still at extraordinary levels, right? So I think we are still -- we have still good reasons to be very confident in our business.

Omar Nokta: Yes. I appreciate that. And then maybe just a follow-up and maybe on DHT more specifically, also in your last comments, you talked about the charter market being somewhat of a wide bid-ask spread. You do have a handful of vessels rolling off charter next quarter, especially those ones that you had booked earlier this year on those lucrative 12-month contracts. How are you thinking about what you do with those vessels as they roll off? I mean, clearly, there's a lot of time between now and then, especially in this market.

But as you think about your charter cover and then the ability to extend them, has the charter shown an interest or a willingness to want to extend them? And then from your perspective, what would you rather do, put them on the spot, keep them on contract on a fixed rate basis or profit share sound more agreeable?

Svein Moxnes Harfjeld: As you pointed out, Omar, there's still some time left for this to happen. So it's too early to say. So that's -- it's too early to say.

Operator: [Operator Instructions] And our next question comes from the line of Jon Chappell from Evercore ISI.

Jonathan Chappell: Svein, a little help maybe with the profit share on your time charters. If I look at the fleet list on your website, it's just 2 ships with profit sharing Appaloosa and Puma. But the amount that you're earning on the time charters, especially if we look backwards to the third quarter, makes it seem like it's more than just 2, just trying to work on the math. So are there any other profit sharing arrangements that aren't listed on the fleet tab? And if not, can you at least help us with the terms of those current 2 to try to rectify some of the math on that time-charter rate?

Svein Moxnes Harfjeld: So thank you, Jon. There are only 2 ships with profit sharing mechanism on the time-charter fleet. I think the numbers you alluded to reflect some -- that these structures have been very rewarding for DHT. But we are not disclosing the details of these structures. So I think you just need to look at the number, and I think this is good for the shareholders in all simple -- that's my simple answer really.

Jonathan Chappell: So the Puma expires in the first quarter of next year, which would leave just the Appaloosa, a, confirmation of that? And then b, are there other opportunities out there to create similar structures? It feels like everything that you signed in the last year or so has been a fixed rate, and you have to kind of balance taking advantage of an incredible time charter rate with the exposure of an even more incredible spot market. So just wondering if that type of activity has dried up in the chartering world just given the extraordinary circumstances.

Svein Moxnes Harfjeld: Yes. So you're right on the Puma, that's expiring in the first quarter. And I think as you allude to here, the extraordinary sort of freight environment makes it difficult to implement profit sharing structures, at least in the way they have been done. Maybe there are different ways to skin the cat, so to say. So we will, of course, look at that if that's possible. 10 seat choices and habits that affect your health on a flight

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

The healthiest seat on a plane depends on your goal, from fewer close contacts at the window to easier movement and circulation on the aisle

The seat that keeps you farthest from a coughing stranger is not the seat that does the most for your circulation. That tension sits at the center of any search for the healthiest seat on a plane. A window seat puts the cabin wall on one side and leaves a passenger with a neighbor on only one side. An aisle seat gives direct access to the aisle for standing, stretching and walking. Both choices protect something. Neither protects everything.

Seat selection affects more than comfort or price. It shapes how much contact a traveler has with other people, how often they get up and how well they sleep. Each position carries its own mix of exposure, movement, noise and light. The right choice depends on what a passenger is trying to protect on a given flight.

Some conditions apply to every seat in the cabin. Most large commercial jets mix cabin air with outside air and pass it through HEPA filters. Those filters remove at least 99.97 percent of particles of 0.3 microns. Cabin air generally flows in a mostly vertical pattern rather than along the length of the plane. That pattern limits how far airborne particles spread down the cabin.

Cabin pressure is shared, too. On most commercial flights, it is equivalent to an altitude of roughly 6,000 to 8,000 feet. No seat assignment changes that.

What does change from row to row is proximity. A passenger's neighbors, the people walking past and the activity around lavatories and galleys all depend on where a seat sits. So does access to the aisle, the amount of legroom and the motion felt during turbulence.

The 10 slides that follow cover a selection of seat positions and in-flight habits worth weighing before booking. They include the window, aisle and middle seats, rows near the front and over the wing, and seats beside lavatories, galleys, exits and bulkheads. They also cover seat choice for sleep and jet lag, along with habits that travel with a passenger to any row. Each one explains the trade-off involved. That lets travelers match a seat to their own priorities, whether the trip is a short hop or a long-haul journey.

Window-seat passengers had fewer contacts with potentially infectious people than passengers in middle or aisle seats in a 2018 model of transcontinental flights. For a traveler whose priority is limiting close contact, the window is the seat that model favors.

The research came from Emory University and Georgia Tech. Vicki Stover Hertzberg led the team. The work was published in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences. The researchers modeled how infection could spread through the cabin on that type of flight.

Position in the row sets how many people sit within reach. A window passenger has one seatmate, with the side of the aircraft on the other. A middle passenger sits between two people. An aisle passenger shares an armrest with one neighbor and an open walkway with everyone else.

That walkway is where the gap widens. In the study, aisle passengers had more contact with people walking past them. Boarding passengers, travelers heading to the restroom and crew members all use the same narrow path. Each pass brings a stranger briefly close to the aisle seat. The window seat sits two places away from that traffic.

Window passengers also have fewer reasons to leave their seats. They have no direct access to the aisle. Every trip out means asking at least one neighbor to stand up or shift.

That habit carries a cost. Moving around during a long flight helps lower the risk of deep vein thrombosis, a blood clot that usually forms in the legs. A passenger who rarely leaves the window seat gets less of that movement. The window favors limiting contact, not circulation.

The study's scope has limits. It was a model of how infection could spread, not a count of confirmed infections among real passengers. It also covered only transcontinental flights. Its results do not automatically carry over to other routes, aircraft or flight lengths. A window seat reduced close contacts in that model. It does not make a passenger immune to an illness on board.

An aisle seat is the simplest way to get up and walk during a long flight without climbing past a neighbor. That access matters for circulation. The U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention warns that travel lasting four hours or more raises the risk of blood clots.

The main concern is deep vein thrombosis, or DVT. It is a clot that forms in a deep vein, most often in the leg. Sitting still for hours slows blood flow in the lower legs. That gives a clot a chance to form. The danger grows if part of the clot breaks loose and travels to the lungs. That event, called a pulmonary embolism, can be life-threatening.

For long trips, the CDC advises travelers to move around, stretch their legs and stay hydrated. An aisle seat removes the main obstacle to the first two. A passenger can stand, walk toward the back of the cabin and return without waking anyone or squeezing past a lowered tray table.

That ease can change behavior. Passengers in window and middle seats may stay put for hours rather than disturb the people beside them. Someone on the aisle can get up whenever the seatbelt sign is off, as often as needed.

The aisle also helps with leg exercises done while seated. The CDC suggests raising and lowering the heels with the toes on the floor, then raising the toes with the heels down. An aisle passenger can also extend a leg into the walkway when no cart or person is passing.

Risk is not equal for everyone. The CDC lists factors that raise clot risk, including older age, obesity, recent surgery or injury, pregnancy, estrogen-based birth control and a previous clot. Travelers $TRV in those groups gain the most from a seat that makes moving easy. The CDC also suggests they talk to a doctor about compression stockings before a long trip.

The trade-off is traffic. Aisle passengers sit beside everyone walking up and down the cabin, including crew with service carts and people heading to the lavatory. Elbows and shoulders on the aisle side get bumped more often. A traveler focused on circulation rather than distance from others may accept that cost in exchange for the freedom to move.

A middle seat puts a passenger within arm's reach of two people at once, one on each side. That position matters because exposure to an infectious traveler is concentrated in the seats closest to that person.

Researchers at Emory University and Georgia Tech studied this pattern in 2018. Their study, led by Vicki Stover Hertzberg and published in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, modeled how infection could spread on transcontinental flights. It found that the highest risk of exposure fell within about one row and one seat of an infectious passenger. Most passengers seated farther away had little exposure.

A middle seat falls inside that zone for both neighbors in the same row. If either the window passenger or the aisle passenger is sick, the person in the middle sits directly beside them for the length of the flight.

The study modeled one type of flight. Its findings describe transcontinental routes, not every trip. The seating geometry holds on any flight. In a row of three seats, the middle seat has two direct neighbors, while the window and aisle seats each have one.

Movement is the second issue. A middle-seat passenger cannot reach the aisle without asking the aisle neighbor to stand or squeezing past them. That makes getting up during a long flight more awkward. Each trip out of the row also brings the passenger face to face with a seatmate in a tight space.

A traveler assigned a middle seat has a few practical options. Checking the airline's seat map after booking can show whether a window or aisle seat has opened up. Many airlines also allow seat changes at check-in, when the map can look different from the one shown at booking.

For passengers who stay in the middle, timing makes the row easier to manage. Standing up when the aisle neighbor is already out of their seat avoids asking them to stand again. A middle seat in a row with an empty neighbor seat also removes one of the two direct contacts.

A seat near the front of the cabin mainly shortens the time spent standing in a crowded aisle at the end of a flight. The benefit is practical rather than medical: fewer minutes packed close to other passengers, and a faster return to open space.

The gain is clearest after landing. Passengers usually leave the aircraft in rough row order, starting nearest the forward door. A traveler in one of the first rows can often reach the jet bridge while people farther back are still waiting to stand.

That wait is one of the most crowded stretches of any trip. People stand in the aisle, pull bags from overhead bins and lean over seated neighbors. Sitting near the front reduces how long a passenger spends in that queue.

Boarding works differently. Many airlines board premium cabins and status holders first, then call economy passengers in groups. Depending on the method, a forward economy seat can mean walking past fewer seated passengers to reach the row. It can also mean a later boarding group. By the time that group boards, the nearby overhead bins may already be full.

Boarding procedures differ by airline and sometimes by aircraft. A carrier's posted boarding order shows whether a forward seat actually means less time in the aisle on that flight.

A forward seat also helps on a tight connection. Getting off the aircraft sooner leaves more time to reach the next gate. That can mean walking through the terminal instead of hurrying with carry-on bags.

The location has limits. A front-row seat is not evidence of cleaner surfaces, better air or lower exposure to a sick passenger nearby. Its advantage is less time in a congested aisle, mostly after landing.

Some airlines sell forward economy rows as preferred seats for an extra fee. The cost varies by carrier, route and fare. Seat maps shown at booking usually mark which rows carry a surcharge.

Seats over the wing sit close to an aircraft's center of gravity, where turbulence is usually felt less than in the rear of the cabin. For travelers prone to motion sickness, a row above the wing can make the ride feel steadier.

The reason lies in how a plane moves. When rough air pushes on an aircraft, it tends to pitch nose-up and nose-down around its center of gravity. Rows near that pivot point travel a shorter vertical distance than rows near the tail. Passengers at the back feel the same jolt as a larger swing.

Aircraft are designed so that the center of gravity falls close to where the wings generate lift. That puts the steadiest part of the cabin roughly in line with the wings. No seat removes turbulence entirely, and a row over the wing still moves when the air is rough.

Motion sickness develops when the inner ear senses movement that the eyes do not see. Inside a cabin, seatbacks and overhead bins look still while the body registers each rise and drop. Smaller movements leave less of a gap between those two signals.

A window seat in an over-wing row adds another tool. Looking out at the horizon gives the eyes a fixed reference that lines up with what the inner ear feels. The wing blocks part of the view below, but the horizon usually stays in sight.

There are trade-offs. On many narrow-body jets, including the Boeing $BA 737 and Airbus A320 families, the engines hang beneath the wings. Rows beside them can pick up more engine noise than rows farther forward. Over-wing rows on these aircraft also often include emergency exits, which come with their own seating rules.

A steadier seat does not replace a fastened seatbelt. The Federal Aviation Administration advises passengers to keep their belts buckled whenever they are seated, even after the seatbelt sign goes off. Turbulence can arrive without warning, including in clear air with no visible clouds.

Rows next to an airplane lavatory see more people passing through and standing nearby than most other rows. They also take on more light and noise from the door and the toilet itself. Those conditions make a lavatory-side row a poor fit for passengers who want to rest or keep their distance from others.

On single-aisle jets such as the Boeing $BA 737 and Airbus A320 families, lavatories usually sit at the front and back of the cabin. Wide-body aircraft often add more lavatories in the middle sections. The rows directly beside those lavatories see the most bathroom traffic on any given flight.

Foot traffic near a lavatory is not just people walking by. Passengers often wait in the aisle for the door to open. They can stand beside the closest seats for minutes at a time. On a full flight, a short queue can form right next to someone trying to sleep or eat.

Each visit brings its own disruptions. The door opens, shuts and latches every time. Light from inside the lavatory spills into the cabin when the door opens during a darkened overnight flight. Most modern airliners use vacuum toilets. The flush is loud enough to carry into nearby rows.

The row directly in front of a rear lavatory has an added drawback. Seats backed against a wall or partition often recline only partly, or not at all. Airline seat maps show where each lavatory sits. That makes these rows easy to spot before booking.

The same location has a clear upside for some travelers. Passengers who are pregnant, managing a medical condition that requires frequent bathroom use or traveling with small children may value being a few steps away. Anyone drinking plenty of water on a long flight will also make more trips.

For those travelers, an aisle seat a few rows from the lavatory can be a middle path. It keeps the walk short without placing them in the line of every queue and door cycle.

Exit-row seats generally give passengers more legroom than standard economy rows, but they come with eligibility rules. Bulkhead seats also have no seat in front, though a fixed armrest can make them narrower.

That extra space matters most on a long flight. A passenger with room to straighten the knees and flex the ankles can change position often without standing up. Tall travelers, and anyone who stiffens in a cramped row, gain the most from that room.

The trade-off with an exit row is responsibility. Anyone seated there must meet requirements set by the airline and the aviation authority. That includes being able to help open the exit and assist others in an emergency. In the U.S., these rules come from the Federal Aviation Administration.

Under FAA rules, a passenger in an exit row must be at least 15 years old. The passenger must also be able to understand crew instructions and carry out the exit duties. Flight attendants confirm this before departure. A passenger who cannot or does not want to help can be reseated.

Exit rows also limit what can stay at a passenger's feet. Bags and loose items cannot block the path to the exit. Carry-ons usually go into the overhead bin for takeoff and landing. Some exit-row seats, and some seats directly ahead of an exit row, have limited or no recline.

Bulkhead seats sit directly behind a wall, curtain or partition rather than another row of passengers. With no seat in front, nobody reclines into the passenger's space. Getting out to the aisle can also be easier. There is no seatback to squeeze past.

The design brings its own compromises. The tray table usually folds out of the armrest, and that armrest is fixed in place. A fixed armrest can make the seat narrower than a standard one. That difference matters for wider travelers. There is also no under-seat storage ahead, so bags go overhead during takeoff and landing.

On many long-haul aircraft, bulkhead rows are where airlines fit bassinets for infants. That can mean more activity nearby on overnight flights.

Exact legroom and seat width vary by aircraft type and airline. The seat map for a specific flight shows where the exit rows and bulkheads sit before a traveler commits to one.

Rows beside an airplane galley sit next to the cabin's working kitchen. Those seats pick up more noise, more light and more crew movement than most others. That activity is the main trade-off of the location.

The galley is where flight attendants store service carts, heat meals, brew coffee and prepare drinks. On most single-aisle jets, galleys sit at the front and rear of the cabin. Many wide-body aircraft add a galley between cabin sections. That arrangement places some mid-cabin rows beside one too.

Noise arrives in bursts tied to the service schedule. Carts roll out and lock back into their bays. Drawers and storage compartments open and shut. Crew members talk through each round of service, and the sound carries from setup through cleanup.

Light is the second factor. A galley usually stays lit while the crew works, even after the main cabin lights are dimmed for an overnight flight. Some aircraft have a curtain between the galley and the nearest seats. A curtain softens the glare but does not block sound.

Crew traffic adds a third layer. Meal and drink carts typically begin their run at a galley. That places nearby rows at the start or end of each service pass. Flight attendants also walk past these rows often to restock supplies and collect trash. On long-haul flights, passengers sometimes stand near the galley to stretch. That brings more people close to the adjacent seats.

Food smells come with the location as well. Meals are heated in galley ovens, and the aroma reaches the closest rows first. Travelers $TRV prone to nausea may want to weigh that alongside the noise.

How much these trade-offs matter depends on the trip. On a short daytime flight, galley activity is mostly background. On an overnight route, it can interrupt the hours a traveler planned to sleep.

Airline seat maps and booking sites usually mark where galleys sit on a given aircraft. Checking the map before choosing a seat shows which rows border the galley. Moving even a few rows away puts more distance between a passenger and the busiest stretch of the cabin.

For sleep on a long flight, a window seat offers two practical advantages. It provides a fixed cabin wall to lean against, and it gives the passenger control of the window shade, which governs how much daylight reaches the eyes.

The side panel supports the head and shoulder in a way an aisle or middle seat cannot. Passengers in those seats either sit upright or end up leaning toward a stranger. A travel pillow also holds its shape better when it is braced against a solid surface.

A window seat removes one common cause of broken sleep. No neighbor needs to climb past to reach the aisle. A sleeping passenger is therefore less likely to be woken partway through the flight. The trade-off runs the other way, too. A window passenger who wants to get up has to wake someone else.

The shade matters for more than comfort. Light is the main signal that sets the body's internal clock, known as the circadian rhythm. Bright light suppresses melatonin, the hormone that helps bring on sleep. Darkness allows melatonin levels to rise.

That makes timing useful for jet lag. Light in the morning tends to shift the body clock earlier. Light in the evening tends to push it later. A traveler flying east generally needs to move their clock earlier. One flying west needs to move it later.

Opening or closing the shade to match the light schedule a destination calls for can begin that adjustment before landing. Eastbound trips are usually harder on the body than westbound ones. The human body clock tends to run slightly longer than 24 hours, which makes lengthening the day easier than shortening it.

Shade control has limits. Cabin crew on some flights ask passengers to lower shades so the cabin stays dark for sleep. The Boeing $BA 787 replaces pull-down shades with electronically dimmable windows that crew can adjust centrally.

Screens add a second light source close to the face. Seatback monitors and phones give off light that can delay sleepiness. Dimming them or switching them off helps when the goal is rest.

Recline deserves a check before booking. Seats in the last row of a cabin often sit against a wall or partition and may recline little or not at all. Reviewing the seat map for the specific aircraft can help a traveler avoid those rows.

What a passenger does during a flight affects health risk alongside where they sit. Clean hands, steady water intake and regular movement help in every seat in the cabin, from the window to the aisle.

Many travelers handle the surfaces within reach of any seat over a day of flying. Tray tables, seatbelt buckles, overhead air vents and seatback touchscreens are among the most frequently touched. The seatback pocket and armrests see heavy use too. A passenger has no way of knowing how recently any of them was cleaned.

Hand hygiene links all of these surfaces. Washing with soap and water reduces the chance that something picked up from a shared surface reaches the eyes, nose or mouth. An alcohol-based hand sanitizer is a practical substitute when a sink is out of reach. The moments that matter most are before eating, after using the lavatory and after handling shared items.

Disinfecting wipes add a second layer. Wiping the tray table, armrests, buckle and screen after sitting down takes a minute or two. That covers the surfaces a passenger will touch most for the rest of the flight. Keeping hands away from the face between cleanings serves the same purpose.

Hydration is the second habit. The U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention lists staying hydrated among its recommendations for long trips. Water and other nonalcoholic drinks serve that goal better than alcohol. Alcohol increases the amount of fluid the body loses.

Planning ahead makes this easier. An empty reusable bottle can pass through airport security. It can then be filled near the gate before boarding. That removes dependence on the timing of the cabin drink service.

Movement is the third habit. When the seatbelt sign is off, a short walk to the back of the cabin and back gets the legs working. Passengers who must stay seated, such as during turbulence or a meal service, still have options. They can flex their ankles, press their feet into the floor and roll their shoulders.

None of these habits depends on a seat assignment. A window passenger and an aisle passenger can follow the same routine. The seat sets the starting conditions, and the habits apply in every one of them.

The AI boom could shift diesel generators from backup to everyday use

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Last week’s election results demand community-friendly data centers. Overlooking the role of diesel backup could create more pollution and noise for residents, write experts from Inlyte Energy.

Antonio Baclig is CEO of Inlyte Energy, which develops iron-sodium battery technology. Ben Kaun is Inlyte chief commercial officer and Jon Wellinghoff is a former chairman of the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission and a member of Inlyte’s advisory board.

In October, during what is supposed to be a slack season for the grid, when generators are taken offline for maintenance, a spell of unseasonable fall warmth prompted the U.S. Department of Energy, at PJM Interconnection’s request, to issue an emergency order to avoid blackouts. The event is yet another indication of the strain on grids from demand growth from data centers.

But the Oct. 13-16 event also reveals a crutch that is increasingly being leaned on during grid stress, which may be an unwelcome surprise for communities living next to data centers: the diesel generators that are installed for emergency backup. The DOE’s 202(c) order, consistent with similar orders begun this year around Winter Storm Fern, authorized PJM to direct backup generation at large loads such as data centers to run. Earlier this year, on July 2, as an intense summer heat dome settled over the Mid-Atlantic, PJM recorded its highest-ever unrestricted demand, reaching 168 GW. In Northern Virginia’s Data Center Alley, utility executives said they came within minutes of needing to call on the vast fleets of backup generators installed at data centers to keep the system supplied.

This is a remarkable development. For decades, those backup generators had a simple job: If the grid fails, keep the data center running. Now, as electricity demand grows faster than it has in decades and extreme weather increasingly tests the grid, the generators themselves are beginning to look like a potential source of grid capacity.

That may seem expedient. But this development is happening at the same time as the public backlash against data centers, seen in last week’s mid-term election results, is prompting states to impose requirements for data centers — in September alone, new rules on data centers were signed in Virginia, Massachusetts, Maryland and California. If policymakers and regulators do not pay attention to what is likely to happen during periods of grid strain, increased reliance on diesel may end up being the default for the U.S. grid.

The existence of huge fleets of behind-the-meter diesel generators is a new development brought on by the AI boom. A modern hyperscale data center is no longer simply another large electricity customer. Its electrical infrastructure can resemble that of a small city, with substations, private distribution networks, sophisticated power electronics and enormous amounts of backup generation. Because even a momentary interruption can be extraordinarily costly, data centers install enough backup capacity to keep operating through a major grid failure.

Diesel generators have been the default because they are proven, reliable and relatively compact. Diesel fuel is also extraordinarily energy-dense and can be stored on site. But the generators were designed to sit idle most of the time and operate only during emergencies.

Rapid load growth is changing that equation. New generation and transmission take years to permit and build, while enormous new data center loads can arrive much faster. During periods when the grid is short of capacity, thousands of idle diesel generators can therefore look like an irresistible resource.

Federal emergency authority has already been used to make such backup generation available during periods of severe grid stress. In January, DOE authorized grid operators to tap as much as 35 GW of idle backup generation at large facilities. Similar authority has been invoked 13 times this year, seven of those in PJM.

The danger is that an emergency exception becomes a routine operating model. Once policymakers and utilities begin counting on data-center diesel generators as a source of capacity, there will be less incentive to develop cleaner resources that can provide the same reliability services.

This extracts a local price. Diesel generators produce nitrous oxides, particulate matter and other pollutants. A study of Northern Virginia data centers found that operating their diesel generators up to permitted limits would increase the region’s total nitrous oxide emissions by 45%. The study also found that the census blocks most exposed to those emissions tend to have lower household incomes than other census blocks.

The noise is also disruptive. During a previous electrical emergency in Loudoun County, residents described the sound of data-center generators operating continuously as being like airplanes landing for more than 24 hours.

That may be tolerable as an emergency measure. It is a poor model for the everyday infrastructure of the electric grid.

Virginia is starting to ask the question: its order directs environmental regulators to review the cumulative impact of data center backup generation and to evaluate alternatives including on and offsite battery storage, load flexibility, fuel cells, and other non-diesel options. But will state and federal regulators, and data center companies themselves, take the next steps to prevent diesel generators from being the default solution?

This question is particularly important because data centers are being asked to become more flexible participants in the grid. Recent regulatory initiatives are creating pathways for large new loads to interconnect more quickly if they agree to reduce their demand when the system is short of capacity.

A data center cannot simply turn itself off. However, that does not mean it cannot be flexible. Many strategies are being developed to make data centers appear flexible to the grid, including throttling low-priority compute, shifting compute to other regions, and putting flexible resources such as batteries at the data center site, or nearby, connected front-of-the-meter or behind-the-meter at businesses and homes.

Batteries can reduce the data center’s demand on the grid during periods of extreme system stress. They can absorb electricity when supply is abundant and return it when electricity is scarce.

However, the lithium-ion batteries being installed today have short durations, for example two or four hours. For any longer period of outage or grid stress — and local congestion can create longer periods of stress than the system-wide peaks — if the data center still needs to be curtailed by the grid, where is that power going to come from?

Today, the default is the backup diesel generator, or in some cases natural gas generators. Therefore, once the battery runs out, the data center that is providing a grid resource instantly switches from doing so in a clean way with a battery to doing so in a dirty way with fossil fuels.

It is clear that data center flexibility can use the grid more efficiently, providing faster access to power at lower societal cost. However, there are many details to be worked out. As policymakers and regulators formulate and implement new data center rules, they should look carefully at how data center flexibility is likely to be met and only allow methods that are consistent with the overall goals of states. If they do not, the expediency of diesel generators could result in it being used more often, creating more pollution and noise for local communities.

Looking to the future, the most secure way to ensure that on-site fossil fuel generators are not used for data center flexibility is to not have them there at all. Why not just replace the diesel backup entirely with batteries, as recommended by the Better Data Center Project? A common objection is that the batteries do not provide enough backup duration.

One alternative is simple: long-duration energy storage, either on-site or proximate within the potentially congested area. A storage system capable of providing 24, 48, or even 72 hours of backup could give a data center a high level of resilience, while at the same time allowing the facility to strengthen the grid productively on an everyday basis.

And long-duration storage does not necessarily mean installing more lithium-ion batteries. New battery chemistries, and other clean backup technologies, are emerging that use more abundant materials, are designed for stationary rather than transportation applications, and can potentially offer different combinations of safety, cost, footprint and duration.

For data centers, footprint is particularly important. Land around a hyperscale facility is scarce. Technologies that can safely pack long durations of stored energy into a relatively small area could make it possible to replace diesel backup without requiring any more land. Additionally, LDES could be placed for the benefit of the data center at other consumer locations such as commercial and industrial sites.

The immediate objective should not be to pick a single winning technology today. It should be to make sure that data-center developers and regulators do not assume that diesel generators are the only practical answer, and that they work to incentivize and develop alternative technologies. So far, LDES has been framed as a front-of-meter bulk storage solution, and state incentives have only rewarded behind-the-meter short duration storage. There should be a signal to the data center or other customers that the resilience contribution of behind-the-meter LDES is valued for bulk system reliability and the elimination of local emissions. This could take the form of allowing behind-the-meter LDES to be accredited towards system capacity and state procurement mandates while taking into account the full effective load carrying value of their durations, as well as modifying existing behind-the-meter rebate and demand response incentives to reward longer durations rather than capping them at shorter duration.

Across the country, residents are increasingly questioning the impacts of data centers: their electricity consumption, water use, noise, and local pollution. Some of those impacts may be unavoidable. But many are the consequence of choices about how these facilities are designed.

Virginia and other states are beginning to define what a community-friendly data center looks like. The industry should meet that standard by building data centers that are clean and quiet stewards of their communities from the start, rather than locking yesterday’s infrastructure into tomorrow’s grid.

Nasdaq-listed TON Strategy Company (TONX) reported $13.1 million in third-quarter revenue, primarily from staking rewards on its GRAM treasury, compared with $15 million in the second quarter.

The company held approximately 235.1 million GRAM (formerly Toncoin) worth $348.5 million at the quarter's end, with 232.9 million GRAM deployed in staking and generating an annualized gross yield of approximately 16%.

The company also repurchased $5.7 million of its shares and supported TON network upgrades as Telegram began rolling out its native Gram Wallet.

TON Strategy posted a $24 million net loss, including a $27.2 million unrealized loss on its GRAM holdings, reversing an $82.8 million unrealized gain in the prior quarter. The firm said it made progress toward a more efficient cost structure following the wind-down of inherited VERB operations.

"GRAM is integrated into several existing Telegram services, including advertising payments and creator monetization, and TON supports blockchain-based ownership and transfer of Telegram usernames and gifts," CEO Kevin Wilson said in a release. "We believe expanded native wallet access can make GRAM easier to use for payments and transfers, while opening additional opportunities for consumer applications built on TON."

In a deal led by Kingsway Capital, the now-former Verb Technology Company raised about $560 million in August 2025 to rebrand into a treasury company for The Open Network's native token.

Earlier this year, TON was renamed to Gram, reviving the original branding outlined in Telegram's white paper.

"700K+ self-custodial wallets created on Telegram in the last 48 hours alone. Adoption spreads virally: a wallet activates the moment someone receives Grams. The crypto revolution is going strong," Telegram founder and CEO Pavel Durov posted to X on Monday.

TONX shares were up 2.4% in Tuesday's session, putting their year-to-date gains near 25%.

Anthropic is heading toward Wall Street with a hefty price tag and an even bigger question: how much appetite does the market still have for AI? The Anthropic IPO roadshow has officially begun, with shares expected to price between $70 and $78.

The proposed offering covers 825 million Class A shares. Anthropic is selling 801,351,354 shares, while existing stockholders are offering another 23,648,646. The company also expects to give underwriters a 30-day option for up to 123.75 million additional shares.

The IPO isn’t the only capital event on the table. Anthropic is also set to raise $26 billion through a separate private placement involving cornerstone investors, including Google, Nvidia and even AMD among the list.

The listing arrives as higher borrowing costs, geopolitical turmoil and elevated bond yields weigh on the IPO market. That makes this offering a significant test of investor confidence in the AI boom.

Anthropic has applied to list on the Nasdaq Global Select Market under the ticker ANT. But the registration statement filed with the SEC has not yet become effective, meaning shares cannot legally be sold before that happens.

With SpaceX having raised $75 billion at a nearly $1.75 trillion valuation, comparisons are inevitable. The Anthropic IPO now has to prove that public-market investors are still willing to pay a premium for AI growth.

Why Aging Stars Suffer A Sudden Luminosity Dip

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

From a naked eye perspective, stars are stable and predictable. Not much goes on but shining. The Sun itself is fairly plane and nondescript and will shine relatively steadily for billions of years. It's considered a low-mass star compared to the most massive stars, which are rarer and contain many times its mass.

Powerful telescopes bring millions of stars of different types and ages into view. Astrophysicists and astronomers watch the Sun and its low-mass brethren, and note changes in their shining, just as they do all types of stars. Their observations reveal the changes stars experience during their long lifetimes.

As low-mass stars like the Sun exhaust their hydrogen supplies and evolve, they become Red Giant Branch (RGB) stars. This is not only theoretical, it's an observational conclusion. RGB stars are easy to observe and study for two reasons: they're bright, and they're numerous. Arcturus, Aldebaran, and Pollus are all bright, naked-eye stars and are all RGB stars. (Well-known Betelgeuse is a Red Supergiant, not quite the same.)

As RGB stars age and cool, they swell in size and become more luminous. It's a pretty steady change. But for some reason, there's a brief and abrupt dip in their brightness known as the RGB luminosity bump. The bump is called that because of how it appears in the colourâmagnitude diagrams of stellar clusters.

Astrophysicists know why the bump ends. But what initiates the bump is still unexplained. Astrophysicists don't like this gap in their knowledge and they've been trying to fill it in.

New research in The Astrophysical Journal Letters has an explanation for the bump. It's titled "The Role of Specific Entropy in the Onset of the Luminosity Bump," and the sole author is Saskia Hekker. Hekker is a Professor of Theoretical Astrophysics and the Group Leader of the Theory and Observations of Stars Group at the Heidelberg Institute for Theoretical Studies.

"During their evolution, stars follow a distinct path in luminosityâtemperature space. Low-mass stars that have exhausted hydrogen in their core follow the so-called red-giant branch (RGB), which is predominantly in the direction of increasing luminosity and decreasing surface temperature," Hekker explains. "The luminosity bump is a temporary decrease in luminosity against this otherwise increasing trend."

The inset grey box is the Hertzsprung-Russel diagram for a 1 solar mass star progressing from the subgiant branch to the tip of the Red Giant Branch. The tiny grey box inside it is magnified in the larger part of the image, highlighting the RGB luminosity bump. Image Credit: S. Hekker 2026 ApJL 1008 L61

The bump is understood theoretically and also confirmed with observations. But there's one discrepancy between theory and observation. In models, the bump appears at higher luminosities than the observed bump, meaning it appears later. "To understand why the bump appears later in the models than in observations, the physics of the bump needs to be unravelled," Hekker writes.

Stars, including our Sun, are layered, and RGB stars are no exception. But unlike the Sun, RGB stars have exhausted the hydrogen in their cores, and most of what's left is in their envelopes. Since the core has exhausted its hydrogen by fusing it into helium, the core is inert helium. An RGB's fusion occurs in a thin shell. In both these regions, energy is transported via radiation.

Above this radiative region is a convective outer envelope.

As an RGB star ascends the branch, the core contracts and the surface expands. Since the core is inert helium, it exerts no outward pressure and is forced inward. "This causes the deepest layer of the convective envelope to move inwards, reaching layers that have changed their chemical composition due to (burning) processes earlier in the evolution of the star," Hekker explains.

This is when dredge-up occurs, as deeper layers are forced into the convective zone. Since the convective zone is convective, the material from the radiative zone mixes into the convective zone, changing its composition.

Now there's a situation where there's a shifting boundary deep inside the star called the Base of the Convective Zone (BCZ). It's organized around a thermodynamic property called specific entropy. It basically measures how disordered the gas is at the BCZ. As the BCZ moves deeper inside the star, the entropy difference across the boundary shrinks. Past a certain point, entropy across the boundary layer drops past a critical value, and that triggers the luminosity bump.

This diagram shows the interior of an RGB star (not to scale). An inert helium core is in the center, and it's contained in a thin hydrogen-burning shell. Above that is a radiative zone separated from the star's convective envelope by the base of the convective zone (BCZ). Earlier dredge-up and mixing created this boundary between helium-enriched and hydrogen-rich gas. This work ties the RGB luminosity bump to this boundary. The author argues that the onset of the bump occurs when the entropy across this boundary crosses a critical value. (Image Generated with AI)

Previous explanations for the bump rested on mechanics. In those explanations, the thin hydrogen-burning shell reaches the boundary and something inside the star changes, triggering the bump. In this explanation, the bump happens when an entropy threshold is crossed inside the star's thermodynamics. It's not simply that layers meet.

We can't see inside stars. Astrophysicists use physics to try to understand what's going on there, and they test those physics against the only part of the star they can really see, the outside. It's a constant back and forth to refine theories until they match observations.

âThis succinct explanation of the onset of the bump, is the first quantitative description proposed so far,â said Hekker.

This new work will make its way into the larger body of astrophysical theory.

"This viable description of the mechanism behind the onset of the bump provides crucial and new insights for the exploitation of the bump as an anchor point in stellar evolution theory," Hekker writes. "Such added knowledge of the onset of the bump could potentially be used to make more informed choices for changes to the physics in stellar models that go beyond calibration of the overshoot, to place the bump in stellar evolution models at the correct luminosity while at the same time aligning them with measured surface abundances."

Evan Gough is a science-loving guy with no formal education who loves Earth, forests, hiking, and heavy music. He's guided by Carl Sagan's quote: "Understanding is a kind of ecstasy."

Anthropic, the AI giant behind the Claude chatbot, is preparing for an IPO roadshow, according to an announcement from the company. An IPO roadshow is basically a series of sales presentations to potential investors in order to gauge interest and set a final share price.

Interestingly, Anthropic is looking to go public at a $1.4 trillion valuation. This is lower than investor expectations, which reportedly reached $2 trillion just two months ago.

Anthropic's decision to go forward with an IPO is in sharp contrast to the approach of its largest competitor, OpenAI, which has largely shied away from doing so anytime in the near future.
