Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Our first look at Zen 6, both under the hood and in real-world applications.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

AMD’s EPYC ‘Venice’ CPUs are the first to debut the new Zen 6 architecture, and we’ve been testing two chips from the range over the past several weeks to see how they compare to Nvidia’s Vera, and more broadly, how the Zen 6 architecture departs from Zen 5 ahead of the expected consumer-focused Olympic Ridge launch next year. Zen 5 was a ground-up redesign of AMD’s storied Zen core, and Zen 6 takes that foundation and expands it in just about every regard.

Zen 6 uses TSMC’s N2P node, but it also comes with significant architectural changes under the hood. Within the core, a redesigned vector engine pushes AMD’s SIMD performance even higher, and it now natively supports half-precision FP16 instructions alongside full, 32-bit precision. It also splits integer schedulers within the execution engine, allowing for more instructions in flight through the core.

Across the die, AMD made other significant changes. The single IOD of previous generations is now split across two IODs, giving the SP7 platform access to 16 memory channels (though at the cost of PCIe 6.0 lanes compared to the previous generation and SP8 platform).

The CCDs have been upgraded, too, for the first time since Zen 3. A standard Zen 6 CCD can now scale up to 12 cores, while a Zen 6c packs 32 cores. With Zen 5 and 5c, CCDs topped out at eight cores and 16 cores, respectively. For the Zen 6c CCD, AMD also doubled the amount of shared L3 per core, which effectively quadruples the L3 compared to Zen 5c, since the CCD packs twice the number of cores.

Interestingly, Zen 6 is neither a marginal optimization to Zen 5, nor a complete architecture redesign. Stepping back, it looks like AMD pushes as many dials as it has access to to their highest level, and that holds up looking at performance.

The 256-core EPYC 9996 delivers 1,024 threads in a 2P configuration, which came out 93% ahead of the 768-thread 2P EPYC 9965 server we tested in SPEC CPU 2026’s intrate throughput test. The frequency-optimized EPYC 9686F excels elsewhere, notably in scientific computing and HPC workloads, and it serves as AMD’s competitor to Vera, at least before the LPDDR-based Verano platform arrives from AMD next year.

AMD says it believes Venice is fundamentally a generation ahead of the competition, which would be a worthless line if it wasn’t backed up by data. Intel’s Granite Rapids-AP is rarely in the conversation, and although Vera maintains some competitive positions, Venice is often ahead — sometimes orders of magnitude ahead.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Intel’s Diamond Rapids is set to launch next year, which should help balance the scales. But AMD has set an incredibly high bar with Venice, one that it will likely extend even higher for some workloads with the arrival of Venice-X and Verano next year.

There’s a lot to get to here. We have a full deep dive into the Zen 6 microarchitecture, a broader look at the chip itself, the platform(s) AMD is pushing out this generation, and of course, more than 100 benchmarks covering everything from Python performance to HPC.

The headline for Zen 6 is a 27% average IPC uplift over Zen 5 in enterprise, cloud, and AI workloads. The debut of this new microarchitecture is focused on the data center, so you shouldn’t take that 27% IPC improvement as broadly applicable to all workloads. Additionally, AMD arrived at that number by comparing fixed-frequency Zen 5 to Zen 6c with 16 cores. As we’ll get into in the next section, a Zen 6c CCD can comfortably fit 16 Zen 6c cores with room to spare, while 16 Zen 5 cores are split across two CCDs.

The compute die is built on TSMC’s N2P node, which is an already optimized version of N2 that comes with a 5% performance improvement. Compared to N3E (Zen 5 used N4P), N2P offers an 18% speed improvement at iso-power, or a 36% reduction in power at the same speed.

AMD described Zen 5 as a ground-up redesign when the microarchitecture first broke cover in mid-2024, characterized by a wider execution engine capable of dispatching and returning eight instructions per cycle, a wider frontend to feed it, full AVX-512 support, and doubled bandwidth between the L2 and L1 cache. Zen 6 is a continuation of the redesign we saw on Zen 5. It carries many of the same characteristics, as well as the same general topology, just enhanced in a number of ways.

As we go through the breakdown, keep in mind that Zen 6 and Zen 6c use the same microarchitecture. Zen 6c is just a space-optimized design that consumes less power, clocks lower, and, obviously, takes up less die space than a full Zen 6 core. But, the company uses the same microarchitecture inside the core, unlike Intel’s E-cores, which use a different microarchitecture than the P-cores.

Starting with the frontend, it’s largely unchanged from Zen 5, short of a few notable improvements. You get the same dual 4-wide decode pipes and 8-dispatch, but AMD has improved the BPU and op cache. For the BPU, it has a 64-entry Return Address Stack, up from 52 on Zen 5. AMD also increased the op cache size back up to 6.75K entries from 6K on Zen 5 (Zen 4 also had a 6.75K-entry op cache). This larger cache can serve up to 18 micro-ops per cycle, up from 12 on Zen 5.

AMD split the integer schedulers for Zen 6 (Zen 5 had a unified scheduler), now offering higher capacity, with 96 shared ALU/AGU and 48 dedicated ALU entries, compared to an 88 / 56 split for ALU/AGU on Zen 5. AMD also expanded the general-purpose registers to a 352-entry physical register file, up from 240 on Zen 5.

The vector execution engine is also improved, following on the heels of a true 512-bit register in Zen 5. Zen 6 carries forward that 512-bit data path and adds some additional improvements. Notably, the engine now supports half-precision AVX-512 FP16 instructions, which needed to be converted in Zen 5’s vector execution engine. Also notable is the FP load/store bandwidth. Zen 5 supports two loads of any size, but Zen 6 supports either two 512-bit loads or four 256-bit loads. Store bandwidth is unchanged, at one 512-bit store or two 256-bit stores.

Zen 6 carries forward the 48 KB 12-way L1 data cache from Zen 5, along with 1 MB 16-way private L2. The shared L3 is treated as victim cache for the L2, as is the case in Zen 5. However, AMD increased the in-flight window compared to Zen 5, with an 80-entry load queue and 128-entry store queue.

Perhaps the biggest change going from Zen 5 to Zen 6 comes in the density of the CCD. Zen 6 offers up to 12 cores per CCD, up from 8 on Zen 5, while Zen 6c scales up to 32, double that of Zen 5c. Elsewhere, the updated CCDs either match or improve on Zen 5/5c CCDs.

For Zen 6, AMD retained the 4 MB of L3 cache per core, increasing the total cache to 48 MB to accommodate the new 12-core limit. The maximum frequency of a standard Zen 6 CCD has increased from 5 GHz on Zen 5 to 5.3 GHz on Zen 6. Keep in mind these clocks are in the context of Venice; we expect higher maximum boost clocks on consumer Olympic Ridge.

Zen 6c has more enhancements. AMD doubled the amount of L3 per core from 2 MB to 4 MB, matching the normal Zen 6 CCD. And, with double the L3 per core and double the number of cores, the Zen 6c CCD has a total of 128 MB of L3, quadruple that of a Zen 5c CCD. For Zen 6c, the maximum frequency is also up, going from 3.7 GHz on Zen 5c to 4.1 GHz on Zen 6c.

Across both, AMD claims a “large core IPC uplift,” as well as improved power and SMT efficiency. We haven’t seen an overall IPC claim yet, though the numbers referenced above put Venice at about a 27% advantage over Turin in enterprise, cloud, and AI workloads.

In the context of Venice, AMD can fit up to eight CCDs on a chip, coming out to 256 cores in total for Zen 6c, and 96 cores total for regular Zen 6 for SP7 platforms (the initial lineup for Venice). AMD is moving past the SP5 socket available to Genoa and Turin CPUs for SP7, which is a slightly larger socket at 76 x 80 mm (SP5 is ~72 x 75 mm).

The topology of the chip is largely intact from Turin, short of the dual IO dies that characterize Venice. The two IODs live in the middle of the chip, flanked by Zen 6 CCDs. The second IOD is what gives SP7 chips access to 16 channels of memory per socket (8 channels per IOD), supporting up to 12,800 MT/s speeds using MRDIMMs in a 1DPC configuration. With RDIMMs, AMD supports up to 8,000 MT/s. The platform supports up to 8 TB of memory per socket, and AMD claims bandwidth of up to 1.6 TB/s per socket (1 TB/s per socket with RDIMMs).

Also on the IOD, there’s the hardware root-of-trust, as well as AMD’s confidential computing extensions, including execution encryption, memory integrity, and trusted I/O. New to the platform is PCIe 6.0 at speeds up to 64 Gbps per lane. A 1P SP7 configuration has access to 96 PCIe 6.0 lanes, while a 2P platform has up to 128. Each configuration also comes with “bonus” PCIe 4.0 lanes, either 8 for 1P configs or 16 for 2P.

Two 64G SerDes interfaces bring the PCIe lanes into the IOD, one of which is used to link the chips in a 2P configuration. That second interface can still optionally handle an additional 12 PCIe 6.0 lanes, giving the “up to” 128 2P lanes number. The bonus lanes run through a smaller 16G SerDes interface.

Platforms with the SP8 socket trade memory channels for PCIe lanes. The SP8 socket is smaller at 62.5 x 80 mm, and the platform supports eight memory channels instead of 16, though with the same support for up to 12,800 MT/s MRDIMMs. Memory bandwidth drops to 819 GB/s for MRDIMMs and 512 GB/s for RDIMMs at the maximum supported transfer rate.

However, SP8 chips have access to 64 PCIe 6.0 lanes per IOD, for a total of 128 lanes in a socket (plus the additional eight PCIe 4.0 bonus lanes). Just like SP7, some of those lanes are shared in a 2P configuration, dropping the total to 192 PCIe 6.0 lanes in 2P SP8 servers, with 16 additional PCIe 4.0 lanes. Both platforms support CXL 3.1.

AMD also provided a platform breakdown for its upcoming Verano range, which uses the Zen 6 microarchitecture on an LPDDR5X-based platform, leveraging replaceable SOCAMM2 DIMMs.

AMD has added a few quality-of-life features to Venice CPUs that should make them easier to manage when actually deployed. First is UPP, or Unified Power Provisioning, which is a power management feature that takes into account total platform power, allocating it as needed to different components, depending on the workload. With it, AMD says it’s able to deliver a power consumption of 830W on 16 servers to 706W on 18 servers, or a decrease in provisioned power by 15%, while increasing the number of servers per rack.

Also dealing with power is UBPS, or Utilization Based Power Scaling. It’s a dynamic power scaling feature that aligns with whatever workload is running on the server, which AMD says “helps close the gap between provisioned and consumed power.” Like UPP, UBPS is concerned with platform power, not just the power consumption of the CPU.

The most interesting addition is FAST, or Frequency Adaptive Spectrum Technology. The feature takes in telemetry from the platform and policies set in firmware to adjust the frequency within set power and thermal limits. It sounds a little more involved than a simple Precision Boost Overdrive for the data center, however.

AMD described FAST to us as allowing for two performance tiers within one socket. As an example, AMD pointed to a 96-core Venice CPU, saying FAST allows 32 of those cores to run in a “premium” category, with a base clock of 3.65 GHz and a max clock of 5 GHz, while a “batch” category with 64 cores runs at 2 GHz with a 2.4 GHz max. AMD described it as multiple commercial SKUs within a single socket.

Venice marks the point when AMD will start offering MEXT on its EPYC platforms. AMD aquired MEXT earlier this year, and it has used the feature internally. With HPE, AMD is rolling out “MEXT ready” platforms, which will, eventually, have access to MEXT licenses. The technology is interesting, especially given how constrained memory is right now.

The general concept of MEXT is using flash as DRAM. Even the fastest flash is orders of magnitude slower than DRAM, but MEXT attempts to take advantage of DRAM access patterns to expose more memory to the system without it actually being there. Pages that haven’t been accessed in several minutes are offloaded to flash storage, and an AI model predicts pages that are needed. Then, those pages are loaded back into memory as you need them.

Conceptually, it sounds too good to be true. AMD pointed out that MEXT is using a transformer-based LLM, and effectively, LLMs are predicting what words go next when generating text. Here, AMD says the bar isn’t even that high. As long as the model can stream the correct pages in, and do so fast enough to cover the flash latency, the order of them doesn’t matter.

MEXT is delivered through a Linux kernel module, and AMD has bold claims about its capabilities. The company has deployed the software internally on 145 servers, which it expects to expand to over 2,000 servers next year. Currently, AMD is using it for critical EDA workflows, such as Synopsis PrimeTime. The company says that it’s able to maintain 95% of the application’s performance on Venice with just 50% of the system DRAM. Going down as low as 25%, the company claims the app maintains 80% of its performance.

We’ll have more on MEXT shortly. The feature is exclusive to EPYC CPUs, and AMD was clear that it only applies to certain workloads right now. There are likely applications where you won’t be able to cut the DRAM capacity of a server, or where you’ll need to use a more conservative cut of 25% or less.

AMD gave us access to two Zen 6 Venice systems for the purpose of testing, both of which were dual-socket servers. For the first system, we tested the dense EPYC 9996, which is AMD’s high-core-count design, packing 256 cores and 512 threads per socket, for a total of 512 cores and 1024 threads across a 2P system. It’s a peak throughput SKU, designed to handle heavily concurrent workloads.

On the other end of the spectrum, we have the EPYC 9686F, which is still a high core count CPU in its own right, with 96 cores and 192 threads, giving a 2P system 192 cores and 384 threads. The “F” suffix for AMD’s data center CPUs notes a high-frequency SKU, designed to push performance up in more demanding applications, as opposed to the dense, frontend-focused EPYC 9996.

Both chips use AMD’s SP7 socket, which is geared toward data centers, with higher core count SKUs. The upcoming SP8 socket is focused on enterprise customers, with smaller core counts and support for fewer memory channels.

Although the EPYC 9996 is a dense design with the Zen 6c core, it comes with a fairly high boost clock of 4.1 GHz, though a much lower 2.55 GHz base clock, which it will likely be running closer to in real deployments. It comes with a massive 1,024 MB pool of L3 for the chip, as well as a 600W rated TDP, though it can be manually adjusted between 400W and 600W.

The EPYC 9686F proves out AMD’s claims about Venice, serving as one of two chips in the SP7 lineup to boost up to 5 GHz. It has a lower 500W TDP, though it can again run between 400W and 600W. This is the SKU AMD has heavily referenced in its published Venice benchmarks as a “96-core HF” model.

It’s an interesting model, perhaps more so than the EPYC 9996. It’s in direct competition with Nvidia’s Vera CPU, not only based on core count, but also on performance. These two chips trade blows in several benchmarks, and it’s clear that AMD is pushing this SKU as its “agentic CPU” of the Venice lineup, similar to how Nvidia has positioned Vera.

32 x 64 GB (2TB) SK hynix DDR5-12800 ECC MRDIMM

24 x 64 GB (1.5 TB) SK hynix DDR5-6000 ECC RDIMM

24 x 64GB (1.5 TB) Micron DDR5-8800 ECC MRDIMM

16 x 96 GB (1.5 TB) Samsung LPDDR5-9600 ECC SOCAMM2

You can see the platforms we tested above. The Nigeria reference platform we tested these Zen 6 chips on came with top-end DDR5-12800 MRDIMMs, arranged to fully saturate the 16-channel memory with 32 sticks. This massive jump in memory support is a critical aspect of Venice, particularly for the hotly contested EPYC 9686F and its all-important matchup against Vera.

Vera’s use of LPDDR5 SOCAMM2 memory allows for higher transfer rates than standard RDIMMs in a field-replaceable package, though through a relatively tame eight-channel setup. Vera can get away with 8-channel memory, however, with its topology allowing for massive data movement across the chip. AMD’s distributed design necessitates a bigger memory configuration, with double the channels and access to even higher transfer rates through the use of MRDIMMs.

Starting off our benchmarks is SPEC CPU 2026, the de facto industry benchmark for server CPU performance. As usual with SPEC runs, the numbers below are “estimates.” We gathered the numbers from real SPEC runs, but we haven’t reported them to the SPEC committee, so they remain “estimates.”

For the throughput benchmark suites, intrate and fprate, we ran a number of copies for each application equal to the thread count of the chip. All of our tests were run on Ubuntu Server 26.04 with Linux Kernel 7.0, and we used GCC 15.2 for all but the Venice chips. GCC 16 brings official support for Zen 6, which we used here. However, we did regression testing against GCC 15 and found only minor differences, and nothing that would change our standings.

Using GCC 16 was important to keep our optimization flags equal. We used common GCC flags, including fast link-time optimization and march/mcpu flags to expose the correct processor instruction support. We also used the open-source jemalloc memory allocator across all tested systems.

The overall intrate scores set the tone, particularly for the EPYC 9996. It has access to 1,024 threads in a 2P configuration, multiplying the clear speed improvements in Zen 6 broadly across more threads compared to last-gen’s top-end EPYC 9965. The 9996 scored a massive 93% higher in this test, though that number requires some context.

Both throughput benchmarks in SPEC reward not only how much work is done, but also how quickly it’s done. With access to a much larger thread pool and improvements in Zen 6 broadly, there’s that multiplicative effect when looking at pure throughput. A 2P EPYC 9996 system is 93% faster than a 2P EPYC 9965 system in this benchmark, but not broadly across the board.

The EPYC 9686F provides some context, which is still good news for AMD, just not on the level of doubling the performance of the previous generation. We unfortunately didn’t have access to a 9575F for a closer gen-on-gen comparison, but the EPYC 9755 is a decent proxy, with its 128 cores and relatively high 4.1 GHz boost clock. Compared with that CPU, the EPYC 9686F in a 2P configuration is 19% ahead — not too shabby considering the thread disadvantage.

Critically for AMD, the EPYC 9686F is also 17% ahead of Nvidia’s Vera CPU. Vera punches far above its weight class compared to the x86 competition, as evidenced by its matchup against the 9755 and Intel’s flagship Granite Rapids-AP 6980P.

Flipping over to float performance, AMD did a lot of work on the vector engine in Zen 6, and it shows up in fprate. The differences between the EPYC 9996 and 9686F are smaller, though not in a way where both chips are hamstrung. Rather, the EPYC 9696F is able to push up closer to the 9996, leaving both Zen 6 chips running circles around the field.

AMD more than doubled the score with its Zen 6 chips compared to our Zen 5 touchpoints, which is a massive achievement. The EPYC 9686F excels even more over Vera in fprate with a 40% lead, despite only a minor bump in core count (88 on Vera to 96 on the 9686F). Intel’s Granite Rapids-AP isn’t in the conversation here for raw performance; though, apparently the range is still in high-enough demand that Intel has increased production in the tight CPU environment we’re currently experiencing.

These throughput benchmarks are most representative of how these CPUs will actually be deployed, running multiple copies of applications under high concurrency. However, SPEC CPU also includes its speed suite, which looks at application performance when you can dedicate the whole chip to running just that one application, more along the lines of a client CPU benchmark.

The EPYC 9686F naturally looks better here with its blistering 5 GHz boost clock. In the integer suite, the frequency-optimized chip is 21% ahead of Vera, both in a 2P configuration. The 9996 still holds up, only falling short of the 9686F, despite much more inter-CCD communication.

SPEC shows the various improvements in Zen 6 showing up in a compounding fashion. There’s the new microarchitecture with an optimized vector path, but also more cores per CCD. And also support for higher memory speeds, as well as more memory channels. In isolation, any of these improvements are solid (if rote) for typical gen-on-gen improvements. Add them up in a workload that can take advantage of all of those improvements, however, and Zen 6 is blistering.

Two of the biggest performance vectors in the agentic era are memory performance and Python performance, at least at the moment. Agentic frameworks are largely defined by Python between LangGraph and CrewAI, though alternative frameworks written in TypeScript and Rust have started to appear as well. Regardless, Python remains important for agentic CPUs right now.

Similarly, memory bandwidth is important for certain types of agents, particularly those that need to query large databases, regardless of if they’re a traditional relational database written in SQL or a non-relational database.

Starting with the industry-standard STREAM benchmark, AMD is ahead of Nvidia’s Vera by between 11% and 19%, depending on the test. However, this was with the OpenMP “true” policy for process binding, which binds processes to the thread they’re placed on to avoid them jumping around and skewing the results. Vera performs well, even with this policy, but its mesh cache and monolithic design allow it to realize big performance gains with the “spread” policy.

This policy also binds processes, but it distributes them across the chip as far away from each other as possible. AMD chips don’t see an improvement with the spread policy, and Intel’s Granite Rapids-AP sees a slight regression. Vera, however, remains on top of the pile, with a clear victory margin with the policy applied.

In the tried-and-true MBW benchmark, the pair of 9996s claim a small lead over Vera, with a small array size of just 128 MB, but Vera jumps into first place when the array size increases. AMD has certainly tightened up the memory matchup against Vera with 16 channels and support for 12,800 MT/s MRDIMMs, but Vera’s topology and already competitive memory subsystem still wins the day.

The clear differences in memory performance flatten off as we flip to Python, which is a much more hotly-contested area of performance. Starting with a broad overview, we have PyBench. Across the suite, the EPYC 9686F was 32% faster than Vera, while the EPYC 9996 naturally claims a smaller 14% lead with its relatively limited boost clocks. For gen-on-gen comparisons, the EPYC 9996 is an impressive 24% ahead of the EPYC, while the 9686F claims a lead of 35% over the 9755 – though, no small part of that advantage is the 900 MHz higher boost clock that the 9686F has access to.

PyPerformance allows us to go more granular into specific Python applications and functions, but a similar pattern appears. The rankings of other CPUs change, but across the vast majority of tests, the EPYC 9686F leads by a clear margin. It makes sense, too, given that Python heavily leans on a single thread at stock.

There are some exceptions, notably the networkx benchmark, where the throughput-optimized 9996 tops the charts, matching Nvidia’s Vera. Despite how impressive Nvidia’s chip is, considering the company’s infancy in data center CPUs and the chip’s tame spec sheet, Venice shows that the CPU game is AMD’s to win (or lose).

Breaking out of the agentic bubble, AMD has continually proven itself as the leading force in general-purpose data center applications, and that trend continues with Venice. As with all of the workloads here, they can touch on agentic AI depending on what the agent is set up to do; database performance, for example, is important if an agent is often querying a database. However, we’re going to keep our analysis at a high level here, as EPYC CPUs will be deployed for a broad range of workloads, some portion of which won’t come remotely close to agentic AI.

Starting with compression performance, AMD generally sits at the top of the charts for compression and decompression speeds, which is the case here with zstd and 7-Zip. In zstd, Vera claims an early lead at the lowest compression level. However, its poor decompression performance shows up even at level 3, and AMD takes over with a clear victory at the compression level 19, with Vera falling even further behind in decompression performance.

The long mode results are especially interesting. Although the rankings don’t change, Vera’s performance in these sustained compression workloads gets worse, while AMD is able to hang onto much more of its performance. Vera and the EPCY 9686F are within noise of each other in the level 3 compression test, and Vera falls to the bottom of the rankings when looking at decompression.

7-Zip has been, for years now, AMD’s game. The charts really speak for themselves here; we’re dealing in multiplicative factors compared to Vera, not to mention the weak competition from Intel’s Granite Rapids-AP. AMD is always strong in 7-Zip, which is compounding here considering the already high base level of performance we see out of Zen 6 across a wide range of workloads.

Onto rendering, Blender shows big gen-on-gen gains for Venice, with the EPYC 9996 offering somewhere between a 30% and 40% improvement over the EPYC 9965 depending on the scene. This is true even in demanding scenes like Barbershop, where render time can be expressed as minutes instead of seconds for many chips. The EPYC 9996 completed the render 35% faster than the EPYC 9965. The EPYC 9686F was able to match the dense EPYC 9965, despite packing half of the cores. Vera isn’t included in our Blender results because a native Arm version isn’t available.

Vera retains a top position in C-Ray, both at 4K and 1080p, though a dual EPYC 9996 configuration is within spitting distance. The script flips in OSPRay, with AMD pulling off clear leads in real-time ambient occlusion, scientific visualization, and path tracing. There are some massive jumps, with the EPYC 9996 nearly tripling the performance of the Xeon 6980P and Vera in the ambient occlusion and scientific visualization tests.

The reworked vector engine in Venice shows up even more clearly in the dim_512 tests, which further stress both the vector engine and memory bandwidth of the platform.

We run three Java suites to measure the ever-important programming language that shows up everywhere from scientific computing to database workloads. Starting on the former end, Java SciMark shows the EPYC 9686F in a clear lead over the competition, with a massive 92% lead over Vera in the composite score. For an apples-to-apples gen-on-gen increase, the EPYC 9996 managed a respectable 24% jump over the EPYC 9965.

Java SciMark provides a nice overview of some critical functions, such as Monte Carlo risk analysis that’s applicable to financial institutions. DaCapo and Renaissance are much wider suites measuring a range of Java-based applications. Many of these tests are highly specific, so the rankings jump around, depending on what the workload stresses. We can see a bit of that with Java SciMark, as well, with Vera claiming the top slot in FFTs. Venice, however, remains ahead.

In large column-based database workloads like Clickhouse, Venice also looks impressive, though not from the jump. Vera maintains its performance from a cold cache, while none of the x86 competition reach steady performance until the cache is warm. The reason should be obvious given Vera’s topology and its distributed mesh, which allows it to jump straight to its peak performance, even when the cache is cold.

Once it’s warm, the 9686F jumps into the lead. There’s a regression in performance going from the 9965 to the 9996, which makes sense for Clickhouse, as larger core counts can create issues in cache coherency.

To look at NoSQL performance, we have simdjson, which leverages SIMD instructions for JSON parsing, as the name implies. Once again, the vector engine in Venice shines, regardless of the workload you look at.

Vera showed strong compilation performance against AMD’s Turin chips, but Team Red leapfrogs that performance with Venice. Starting with LLVM, the frequency-optimized 9686F earns a top slot with makefiles, while the EPYC 9996 tops with Ninja. The drop is around 19% gen-on-gen, aligning with the performance we’ve seen with other workloads.

In PHP, where Vera is more competitive, the EPYC 9686F squeezes out the most marginal of victories, but perhaps most telling is the 9996, which is in the upper tier of chips, despite how dense it is. Building applications, the Godot game engine and ImageMagick once again show both Venice chips on the top of the stack, outclassing the performance of Vera, which previously held a top slot in ImageMagick, and only fell slightly behind the 9755 in Godot.

The 9686F shows large performance jumps in HPC workloads, which AMD says is the target of these lower-core, frequency-optimized SKUs. In CloverLeaf, the EPYC 9686F is more than twice as fast as Vera, and it’s nearly twice as fast with FFTE. In Octave, Vera retains its top position, but both Venice chips are only slightly behind.

Although not traditional HPC, the heavily threaded number crunching behind the scenes in the Stockfish chess engine allows the 1,024 threads in a 2P EPYC 9996 server to outclass the 9965 by nearly 50%. Primesieve shows similarly large leaps, with the EPYC 9996 calculating some 37 billion prime numbers in less than half a second.

AMD already had a strong position in the newly reinvigorated CPU market with Turin. The unique topology and strong focus of Vera has allowed Nvidia to make inroads in certain workloads, but even looking at two SKUs from AMD’s broad Venice lineup, it’s clear that AMD is able to push performance much higher across the board. In some cases, that’s by orders of magnitude gen-on-gen.

There isn’t one area of Zen 6, or the SoCs available with Venice broadly, that totally accounts for the performance increase. It’s small advantages throughout the design, pushing an already strong vector engine further, vastly increasing CCD density, and giving memory bandwidth a boon between support for 12,800 MT/s MRDIMMs and 16-channel memory courtesy of the dual IODs.

But the story AMD has been trying to tell, and the one that’s difficult to ignore in the current market of a one-size-fits-all approach we’ve seen from Nvidia and Arm, is that Venice is available in a broad range of different configurations. We tested two chips on SP7, but the enterprise-focused SP8 socket is arriving within the next few months. And next year, AMD is pushing out Venice-X with 3D V-Cache for HPC workloads, as well as Verano, which looks like AMD’s take on Vera with its field-replaceable SOCAMM2 LPDDR5X memory.

Even without those chips, the two SP7 chips we tested already give AMD a leading position among current data center CPUs. Intel’s Diamond Rapids could change that story come next year, but the EPYC 9686F provides top-tier performance with HPC and AI host node performance, while the EPYC 9996 provides unmatched throughput.

Jake Roach is the Senior CPU Analyst at Tom’s Hardware, writing reviews, news, and features about the latest consumer and workstation processors.

A closer look at Verano, AMD's first step toward a Zen-based superchip to rival Nvidia's Vera Rubin

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Cores, clocks, memory, and interconnects all perfectly balanced for a singular mission: keeping instinct fed.

AMD's upcoming Verano processors aren't like any Epyc CPUs we've seen before. Rather than general-purpose workloads, Verano is designed specifically to power AMD's next generation of GPU servers.

In an interview with The Register during a press event in Las Vegas last week, Madhu Rangarajan, CVP of AMD's compute and enterprise AI products business, described Verano as being the first step towards building something that more closely resembles Nvidia's Vera Rubin superchips.

"Verano is really designed for an integrated CPU+GPU rack scale solution," he said. "From that perspective, I wouldn't say the CPU is a competitor to Vera. In a head note, I'd say the Verano+GPU solution is a competitor to Vera+Rubin."

For reference, Vera Rubin and the GB200 and GB300 that came before it aren't your typical server platforms. Instead they're more like an enormous single-board computer combining CPUs and GPUs into a tightly-integrated package. Each of Nvidia's superchips joins a single 88-core Vera CPU and 1.5 TB of LPDDR5X memory to a pair of Rubin GPUs via Nvidia's speedy NVLink-C2C interconnect.

Rangarajan tells us Verano will be the host CPU for a similar class of product using AMD GPUs, and much like Nvidia's superchips, the CPU will also use notebook memory rather than conventional RDIMMS.

However, unlike Vera, which is also being deployed as a standalone CPU in storage servers and agent sandboxes, Verano has a singular purpose: keeping its GPUs fed. (We assume they'll be MI500X GPUs.)

This design choice reflects AMD's philosophy of one SKU per workload. As such, Verano's core count, clock speeds, memory bandwidth, power, and I/O have been rebalanced to keep its GPUs fed and not much else.

If we were to peel back Verano's heat spreader, the chip looks a lot like the Venice Epycs we looked at back in September. It features either six of AMD's core complex dies (CCDs) for a total of 72 high-frequency cores clocking to 5.3 GHz, or 192 density-optimized cores clocking to 4.1 GHz, depending on whether the customer's workloads prefer core density or faster clocks.

But that's about where the visual similarities end. Much of the chip has been reworked to eliminate data flow bottlenecks between the CPU and the GPUs it is responsible for feeding. The chip features an entirely custom pair of I/O dies through which four LPDDR5X memory controllers are attached.

Those memory controllers are currently being validated for use with 24 channels of memory at up to 8,533 MT/s. We're told that we may see that creep up a bit depending on how much headroom AMD has when the part starts shipping next year, but for now that's what AMD is validating against.

16 channels of DDR5, 5 GHz clock speeds, and support for CXL 3.1 memory god boxes? What's not to like?

That means the platform not only has fewer cores, but also slower memory. Peak theoretical memory bandwidth tops out at around 800 GB/s, which you might have noticed is half of what AMD's top-specced Venice parts can do and about 66 percent of Nvidia's Vera.

What LPDDR seems to buy AMD is a 30 to 40 percent reduction in power consumption compared to RDIMMS, as well as a more compact footprint for liquid cooling. But it also comes with some downsides. In its first incarnation, Verano won't be socketed. Instead it'll use a ball-grid array (BGA) that's soldered to the board, which will make it harder to service.

"LPDDR tends to be quite finicky in terms of speeds. Every time you go through a connector, you tend to lose a pin or two, and you already have one connector with the SoCam itself. So to minimize any of those losses in speed, we went BGA on the CPU," Rangarajan told us.

Unlike with a lot of notebooks, the memory itself won't be soldered down and will use the same SOCAMM2 modules found in Nvidia's Vera-Rubin and Vera-Vera CPU Superchips.

Even so, Verano looks like a bit of a downgrade compared to Venice except when it comes to CPU-to-GPU connectivity.

Historically, AMD's CPU-to-GPU communication has been done over PCIe. It works but has usually required PCIe switches to keep up with all the GPUs, NICs, and storage on board. Nvidia's superchip dispensed with this, replacing PCIe with its NVLink-C2C interconnect, which provides 900 GB/s of bidirectional bandwidth to each of the system's GPUs.

Venice and Verano do something similar. Unlike past Instinct accelerators, AMD's latest generation of GPUs aren't limited to PCIe for CPU-to-GPU communication. Instead, starting with Venice, AMD transitioned to using xGMI.

Global memory interconnect (GMI) is how AMD has moved data between chiplets on its CPUs, GPUs, and APUs for several generations now. xGMI extends this to GPU-to-GPU and CPU-to-GPU communications.

With Venice, xGMI link bandwidth tops out at 64 Gbps per lane, which the keen-eyed among you have probably already figured out is the same as PCIe 6.0. Verano takes this to another level. As we mentioned earlier, Verano disaggregated the memory controllers, and AMD appears to have reclaimed that space for fatter serializer deserializers (SerDes). Instead of 64 Gbps per lane, Verano's xGMI links top out at 112 Gbps, effectively giving it near PCIe 7.0 levels of bandwidth years before the spec ships in production CPUs.

Rangarajan explains this is driven by what AMD's GPUs need to run optimally.

AMD hasn't disclosed the ratio of CPUs to GPUs it's aiming for with Verano, so it's difficult to say how it compares to Nvidia. In a 2:1 ratio of GPUs to CPUs like we saw in Vera Rubin, Verano would deliver identical chip-to-chip bandwidth, while in a 1:4 ratio like we see in AMD's Helios racks, we'd be looking at about half the bandwidth at 448 GB/s. It's also not clear whether AMD has increased the number of xGMI links per I/O die for Verano or not, so bandwidth per GPU could be even higher. We expect more info closer to the MI500X's debut next year.

AMD doesn't expect Verano to replace Venice in every AI rack: The chip is designed specifically for use with Instinct accelerators.  Moreover, AMD isn't waiting for Verano to roll out its Helios rackscale systems. As we discussed during Advancing AI earlier this year, those systems will feature 18 96-core Venice CPUs paired to 72 Instinct MI455X GPUs.

Moving forward, AMD expects its Venice to remain a popular host processor for AI head nodes not just for its own GPUs but for the growing number of homegrown accelerators built by the great model houses. ®

GTA 6 Preload Is Now Available for All Digital Pre-Orders, Download to Your PS5 Now

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Publisher and developer Rockstar Games has announced that preloading for the highly anticipated GTA 6 is now available to everyone who pre-ordered digitally.

In other words, if you pre-ordered the game via the PS Store, you can now begin downloading and installing the game on your PS5 to ensure it's ready to go come launch day.

The news comes alongside a brief new trailer, which includes a few new snippets of GTA 6 in action as well as the song Blood In Your Eyes by Tove Lo, which is part of the game's licensed soundtrack.

Those who have pre-ordered the code-in-a-box version of the game will need to wait a little longer to begin preloading, and you can read more about that in our GTA 6 Release Times and Preload guide through the link.

It'll definitely be worth preloading the game as soon as possible, because the install size is pretty enormous, so it may take a while for you to get the download done.

Guide GTA 6 Release Times and Preload: When Can You Start Playing?

In other news, Rockstar Games has finally shed some light on what benefits the PS5 Pro brings to GTA 6, the game is confirmed to have gone gold, and a live festival event was also recently announced.

News GTA 6 PS5 Pro Features Revealed, 30fps Target on Both Consoles

Improved clarity and ray traced shadows on PS5 Pro

News GTA 6 Won't Have Any Last Minute Delays, as It's Officially Gone Gold

Google Releases Modern Web Guidance 1.0 For AI Coding Agents via @sejournal, @MattGSouthern

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

This webinar uses Conductor's research to help you prioritize marketing data and AI visibility measurement fixes.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

A free skill set for AI coding agents leaves preview with a codebase audit and public evals. One early test scored higher when agents were told to use it.

Google issued version 1.0 of Modern Web Guidance, which is a free skill set that web development teams may use as an enhancement to their AI coding tools, in order for those tools to write code based on current browser features. The skills are in the form of packaged guidelines that the agent will look at while writing the code. The code generated by the agent will also be sent to the same pages that are evaluated for metrics such as Largest Contentful Paint (LCP), Interaction to Next Paint (INP), and accessibility.

Philip Walton, a senior staff developer relations engineer at Google, announced the release in a Chrome for Developers post. Version 1.0 adds an audit for existing code, and Firefox and Edge are joining the project. Chrome first released the guidance as an early preview at Google I/O in May.

Teams can install it by running npx modern-web-guidance@latest install in a project directory. It’s also available through the plugin marketplaces of most popular coding agents, according to the post.

Version 1.0’s audit will go through a project’s HTML, CSS, JavaScript files as well as its package.json to identify areas where developers are able to safely update their code, then walk the agent through the changes. This first iteration of the audit uses static analysis to look at a limited number of code patterns, according to the post.

The post says the library now targets “hundreds of verified AI knowledge gaps” across its guides. Additionally, current coding models continue to fall short in Google’s tests of accessibility, security headers, and loading priority for scripts and images.

According to Google, each of the guides is now reviewed and approved by a domain tech lead. For user-facing feature guides there is a dedicated accessibility review done by experts who use assistive technologies daily.

A governance document was uploaded to the project’s GitHub repository on Nov. 9 which outlines six owner seats for the project. Two of those seats belong to Chrome, Microsoft Edge and Mozilla each have one, and two are given to independent web platform experts. There is currently one open independent seat and no seat belongs to Apple.

Google’s announcement links to a public eval dashboard where coding agents from Anthropic, OpenAI and Google run the same set of tasks every night, with and without the guidance. A real browser then checks the DOM, styles and source code of each finished app.

When I checked Tuesday morning, guided runs were passing 88% of the automated checks, whereas unguided runs were passing 56%. These figures are based upon each agent’s combined results for nightly runs completed Nov. 8; the range is 199 to 215 tasks per agent. The dashboard provides a limited review (not a full WCAG audit, it says) of accessibility features such as support for keyboards, focus, and form labels.

Its task prompts don’t mention the guidance, so each agent has to work out on its own when a guide applies. In most of the recent suites listed, guided runs ran slower and burned more tokens.

Accessibility consultant Adrian Roselli evaluated the preview in May by giving Antigravity (Google’s AI coding tool) an animated accordion prompt found in the documentation. However, according to Roselli, the output did not comply with WCAG and its animation was not functional in Firefox. He also found no assurance that agents would be able to follow the guidelines. This was for the preview, not v1.0.

The new guidelines were tested by Japan’s creator platform, note inc. using two coding agents that ran through six user interface (UI) tasks across 180 test runs on clone versions of note inc.’s production code base. As explained in a Chrome blog, both agents’ scores on 19 browser checks rose after installing the skill without naming it in prompts, then again once prompts named it. Agent One went from 60.8% to 76.5% to 94.0%. Similarly, Agent Two had scores of 75.8%, 86.2%, and 90.0% under the same three test conditions.

One of note’s tasks checked whether the largest image in view on an article page had high priority and wasn’t lazy-loaded. The comparison table in the post shows that unguided agents wrote hero image code using both high priority and lazy loading.

The guidance is run by MetaMask, as part of an automatic review process for each pull request submitted to its open-source browser extension, according to a second Chrome post. Through Oct. 21 (the first five weeks), the bot identified 102 issues across 57 feature and fix pull requests. The engineers fully resolved 43 of them and partially resolved three of them. Another 16 were either declined or false positives. Twenty of them went unaddressed. The outcome of the other 20 is unknown.

Modern Web Guidance provides a no-cost means for teams to encourage agents writing code to use native browser capabilities and that includes code in existing legacy code bases. Most of the results that have been published are based on testing conducted by Google as well as customer testing that Google highlighted. Note Inc. tested the two agents using the same prompt with and without telling them to use the guidance; they both performed better when the prompt did tell them to use the guidance. The team can also label their prompts to include the guidance and keep field data and manual accessibility testing in the review.

Google says it plans to expand this process of auditing existing code at some point, as well as performing audits in which the agent is reasoning about the actual code being audited.

The company suggests pairing the guidance with Chrome DevTools for agents to allow the agent to run its own created code and test it inside of the browser. Preview users can use the command npx modern-web-guidance@latest update.

Il retroscena su Cisse: c'era anche chi non lo voleva

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

È il milanista del momento senza dubbio. Otto gol, "I love Cisse" e la prima convocazione in nazionale maggiore. Che Alphadjo Cisse in così poco tempo si affermasse in questo modo nel Milan in pochi potevano immaginarlo. Anzi, c'è chi a gennaio non era poi così contento del suo acquisto, per diversi motivi. Alcuni probabilmente anche condivisibili. Ma andiamo con ordine.

Perché è giusto parlare delle giocate e dei gol che stanno facendo gioire i tifosi rossoneri, ma è importante sapere tutto quello che c'è dietro. A gennaio 2026 Cisse sembrava destinato - biglietto di sola andata - al PSV. Una telefonata del Milan ha cambiato tutto: il classe 2006, che stava giocando in prestito al Catanzaro dal Verona, blocca tutto e vuole solo rossonero. Il Diavolo convince il Verona con 8 milioni di euro e convince il ragazzo con un progetto di crescita - probabilmente tra i pochissimi riusciti nell'ultimo periodo - giusto. Si decide che può rimanere in prestito in Serie B, dove stava iniziando ad ingranare, per poi aggregarsi al resto della squadra a Milanello a fine stagione. Si fa tutto in pochissimo tempo: Moncada, ai tempi DT, stravede per lui e Giorgio Furlani chiude velocemente l'operazione col "solito" Busardò, agente del giocatore con la sua CAA Base.

Il retroscena che può raccontare la redazione di MilanNews.it è che in quei giorni di fine gennaio, quando il Milan sembrava poter avere ancora qualche velleità di Scudetto, Massimiliano Allegri prese molto male l'allocamento di parte del budget per andare a bloccare il giovane Cisse, che per di più non sarebbe arrivato in rossonero prima di sei mesi. A gennaio l'allenatore toscano aveva chiesto rinforzi di tutt'altra risma, mentre arrivò solo Fullkrug in prestito dal West Ham. Il mercato sembrava bloccato, bloccatissimo, e un investimento importante per un classe 2006 che non aveva mai messo piede in Serie A aveva totalmente contrariato Allegri: il livornese lo considerava un rinforzo al massimo per il Milan Futuro e non mancò di far filtrare fastidio e negatività per l'operazione poi conclusa dal CEO Furlani.

Da una parte può essere anche comprensibile l'atteggiamento dell'ex allenatore del Milan: a gennaio i rossoneri erano nelle zone alte della classifica e si sognava un clamoroso colpaccio scudetto. Allegri si sarebbe aspettato rinforzi per l'immediato e per competere fin da subito per dare filo da torcere all'Inter, cosa che poi non è successa.

Però, da qui a voler osteggiare l'ingaggio di un giovane potenzialmente molto forte sarebbe stato importante anche trovare una via di mezzo. Cisse alla fine è arrivato e i sei mesi ulteriori in prestito al Catanzaro, nonostante il grave infortunio, probabilmente hanno fatto tutta la differenza del mondo. Gettare l'ennesimo ragazzo nelle fiamme di un Milan in confusione sarebbe stato il solito bagno di sangue. A maggio c'è stato un reset totale da parte di Cardinale che ha azzerato tutto. Il numero uno di RedBird ha poi insistito su come ci dovrà essere sempre più accortezza nel maneggiare i giovani rossoneri: se con Camarda e Comotto siamo ancora work in progress, il percorso di Cisse, ma anche quello di Bartesaghi e Diawara, è un netto taglio rispetto al recente, caotico, passato.

Ora il classe 2006 ha una maglia che ha dimostrato di poter indossare, un allenatore che crede molto in lui - "I love Cisse" estivo di Amorim è già storia - e un propietario attento alla crescita dei giovani. Evidentemente Allegri, pur con le sue ragioni, aveva sbagliato completamente la valutazione. Cisse ha tutto per poter diventare un calciatore veramente importante e non solo un giovane dal bel potenziale. Ma vedendo come parla, come si allena - in estate, quando lavorava a Milanello per recuperare dall'infortunio, rimaneva a guardare il lavoro di Amorim con la squadra per capire subito cosa volesse - e come gioca c'è da essere ottimisti.

Store Timestamps in UTC, Display Them Locally

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A CRM stores '10:30' beside a follow-up task. One representative reads it in Jaipur and another in London. Neither knows which timezone the value represents.

Time data needs to describe either an instant or a local scheduling intention. Those are different requirements.

An enquiry acceptance time is an instant. Store it using a timezone-aware database type or an explicit UTC representation supported by your system.

JavaScript can serialize a valid Date as a UTC ISO string:

This records the browser or server clock's value. For authoritative acceptance time, prefer the backend's timestamp and maintain its clock synchronization.

Do not infer an instant from an ambiguous string such as 06/07/2026 10:30.

Display an instant in an explicit timezone using a locale-aware formatter:

Choose the display timezone from a documented user or application setting. Label it where confusion is possible.

'Call tomorrow at 9 AM in the customer's timezone' contains more than an instant. Store the selected local date and time together with an IANA timezone identifier, then resolve it for execution.

Recurring local schedules need their timezone rules preserved. A fixed UTC offset does not represent future daylight-saving changes.

Some local times occur twice when clocks move backward; others do not occur when clocks move forward. Ask for or document a resolution policy rather than silently choosing an arbitrary instant.

A delayed event's receipt time is not its occurrence time. Avoid overwriting one with another.

Test different viewer timezones, month and year boundaries, daylight-saving transitions for relevant regions, invalid dates, and absent timezone settings.

The conversion code above is illustrative and should be verified in your target runtime. Consistent timestamps make routing, follow-ups, and incident investigation easier because everyone can identify which moment a record describes.

How to answer Claude Code or Codex from your phone

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 10 • Originally published at ponymux.com

You start an agent on the computer at home and leave. An hour later your phone shows that it stopped to ask for permission to run a command. It stays stopped until you answer, and you're nowhere near the desk.

Each of the big agents has a way to answer from your phone. They just don't work the same way:

Use Remote Control, Claude Code's own feature. In a session you've already started, type:

(/rc is the short form.) The session keeps running on your computer, with your files, MCP servers, and settings. Your phone only shows it and sends messages. Open the session by name in the Claude app under Code, or at claude.ai/code. A session that's online shows a computer icon with a green dot.

To be told when it needs you, open /config and turn on Push when actions required. Install the Claude app and sign in with the same account.

The full list is in Anthropic's Remote Control documentation.

Codex does it through the ChatGPT apps, and setup starts on the computer, not in the terminal. You can't set it up from the Codex CLI or the IDE extension.

From the phone you can continue chats, answer questions, approve commands, and read diffs and terminal output. The work still runs on the computer, with its files and tools.

The computer must be awake, online, and signed in. On a Mac laptop, remote access can stay available with the lid open and power connected; with the lid closed it also needs an external display, and choosing Sleep ends it. Each phone is paired with each computer it controls. The rest is in OpenAI's remote connections documentation.

Yes, and it's the one method that doesn't care which agent you run. You connect to the computer, then attach to a terminal where the agent is already running. It takes more setup than the two above.

On the Mac, turn on SSH: open System Settings → General → Sharing, then turn on Remote Login. Under Allow access for, choose Only these users and add yourself.

From outside your home network you also need a way to reach the Mac. Don't open its SSH port to the internet. Use a VPN or a mesh network such as Tailscale, so only your own devices can connect.

Then run the agent inside tmux, which keeps the terminal alive when you disconnect:

Anthropic's own documentation suggests the same for Claude Code over SSH: use tmux or screen so the session survives a dropped connection.

This works, but it's the roughest on a phone. You're typing into a full terminal with a touch keyboard, and the keys an agent needs, Esc, Tab, Ctrl, and the arrows, aren't on it. You also set up the SSH app, the network, and the Mac yourself.

Almost always, the computer went to sleep. Locking the screen doesn't stop an agent; idle sleep does, and so does closing the lid. Remote Control for Claude Code and Codex, and SSH, can only reach a computer that's awake and online.

None of the agents keep the Mac awake between turns, and that's exactly when you're waiting to answer from the phone. How to keep your Mac awake for Claude Code and Codex covers it step by step.

Then there's no single place to answer. Claude Code's remote lives in the Claude app, Codex's in the ChatGPT app, and a Grok session on your own computer has no remote from xAI that I could find in its documentation. Over SSH you can reach all three, but you have to find the right tmux session yourself and read the terminal to see which one is waiting.

With one agent, any of the methods above is fine. With three, the problem is no longer how to connect, it's which one is waiting for you.

Yes. PonyMux runs the real CLI of each agent in its own Terminal, so Remote Control doesn't depend on any agent offering its own remote. It's free on macOS and Windows.

One device controls an agent at a time. On the computer, a Stop button takes control back whenever you want.

What it doesn't do: it doesn't wake a sleeping computer, so Keep Awake is worth turning on before you leave. There's no end-to-end encryption, and no approval cards that answer for you; you read the Terminal and type the answer. The server relays the screen and keystrokes and saves none of them. On the Free plan, one computer shares its agents at a time.

For setup, see Set up Remote Control. Remote Control on your phone covers the key bar and notifications, and How Remote Control stays safe lists exactly what is and isn't protected.

Download PonyMux, free for macOS and Windows: ponymux.com/download

Do this crucial thing to thrive during unemployment

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A job search, whatever the length, is demoralizing.

After losing a job, you’ll start asking yourself challenging questions about finances, childcare and more. You may also feel the pang of an identity crisis—particularly if you’re a longtime employee whose daily life has become tied up with work.

On top of that, there’s the hours of applying, networking, interviewing, and dealing with modern-day job hunt maladies, from AI and ghost jobs. Lack of communication and recruiter bait-and-switch make the process feel useless. 

The mental battle can be a monster. But experts suggest a possible sanity-saving sword to slay that monster: the power of having a routine. That can be key, especially when navigating a process that may drag on past six months or more.

Melissa Dorman, an organizational psychologist and executive coach, shares that in today’s anonymous market, “no matter how much you get done, you can feel behind or get sucked into the vortex of applying for one job after another.”

Crafting (and sticking) to a routine during this period is not only a way to stay positive during a job search—but it could also be a means to open up job seekers to new opportunities.

Routines and productivity

The salve to some of this strangeness may come down to how seekers control what is within their power. A routine can serve as that support and stability that can put a period of uncontrollable unpredictability into perspective. 

“Stability is everything when your world feels like the bottom has fallen out, especially when losing a job and searching for a job in a volatile market,” says Dorman.

By setting routines, what matters is, “you can know how your day will start tomorrow morning.”

As Dorman recommends, try to set specific times for job applications. Outside of that time, focus on developing new skills, conducting networking outreach, or simply giving yourself some grace to reconnect with what brings you joy. These new activities can not only bring you joy—but serve as anchors throughout your day. Over time, you may see that your routine gives you purpose.

“One sanity-protecting exercise is to implement an ‘office hours’ approach for apps,” adds Dorman, who frequently coaches managers on keeping their skills sharp. 

With these kinds of consistent routines, you can free up mental space so you are able to meet the perfect opportunity when it presents itself: “You can’t be productive or maintain motivation long-term if [only] completing job applications is your full-time job.”

Benefits of consistency

Routines can be tricky to make stick, though—the human brain is wired to feel lit up by novel experiences.

“Our brains love new things. When we start a routine, we get a bit of a dopamine hit, or a burst of the motivational chemical that craves rewards. But when the routine becomes familiar, what once felt exciting starts to feel like work,” says Dorman.

That’s why being intentionally consistent is key: blocking off time on your calendar for that 4:30 p.m. jog, or booking a hot desk at your favorite coworking space, where you specifically research interesting companies or dig into building new skills.

With repetition, not only does the craving for novelty become less pronounced, but the routine itself starts to reinforce itself from positive associations. Your weekly workout may suddenly be the most significant time to relax and refresh yourself, for example.

Make time in the first few weeks to feel a little uncomfortable when cementing your new routine—but stick to a regular schedule. And if you’re comfortable sharing your new routine, recruit a close friend to keep you accountable through regular check-ins.

“The more often you do something, the less you have to think about it,” says Dorman.

Being consistent also makes us feel more productive. According to long-cited research by Teresa Amabile, a psychologist and professor emerita at Harvard Business School, the act of making progress on a project of any size involving personal investment leads to feelings of productivity. Her team dubbed this concept the “progress principle.” Amabile’s research shows this ability to make even a small dent on a project improves workers’ motivation, emotional health, and sense of personal performance. The frequency of these feelings also affected the duration subjects remained productive.

In essence, building up wins throughout the day equaled a feeling of motivation for people over the long-term.

Crafting your successful routine

  • 1. Make it a habit


Habits are borne from planning ahead. One place to start when you’re still looking for work? Take an audit of your sleep schedule. Laura Vanderkam, a Fast Company contributor who has written about productive scheduling, stresses the importance of a consistent sleep schedule in her 2022 book, Tranquility by Tuesday.

Unorganized sleep schedules are hidden saboteurs, causing people to crash out and squander productivity in order to make up for rest. It’s too easy to get into a cycle of stretching yourself thin during the work week—only to oversleep on weekends and throw off your rhythm on weekdays. With a consistent sleep and wake-up time, you not only regulate your flow of energy for the day, but you’re setting yourself up for success tomorrow on your terms.

  • 2. Place rewards alongside hard work

Harvard behavioral scientists Katy Milkman, Julia Minson, and Kevin Volpp point to the practice of bundling temptations alongside tedious or daunting work to fuel motivation. Pair activities that are a chore or bore you with a motivational pick-me-up—like your favorite coffee or a few minutes of vinyasa flow.

And Thomas Davies, a previous director at Google for Work and Fast Company contributor, writes that engaging in activities that genuinely light you up will make an onerous or soulless job search feel more fruitful or at least seem like one step to something better.

MIT researchers also draw a connection between the importance of mental hints when developing habits. They claim habits are often associated with a physical cue that triggers a habit response—so go ahead and reach for your noise-cancelling headphones or shift over to your standing desk when hard work calls.

  • 3. Don’t ignore the paths of least resistance

Finally, keeping a routine doesn’t mean being inflexible.

According to a 2026 study carried out by Columbia researchers, people often get stuck in inertia (fixed mindsets, anyone?), which can cause them to disregard new pathways to their goal. Sometimes, the more interesting route can be more fun and lead you to the same result. So stay open to changing your set routines when it feels right. Maybe this means changing the ratio of your networking efforts to application efforts. Maybe this means searching for skills over strict job titles. 

Whatever the shift looks like, the idea applies to before you get the job… and when you land it.

Keep these tips to make your routine a steady one—and to make your unemployment period a productive one.

Nintendo Switch 2 emulation on Android already has The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake up and running

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake now runs surprisingly well on phones after just a week of additional emulator development.

Legend of Zelda fans all over the world have been marveling at the Ocarina of Time remake’s visuals and enhanced gameplay systems on the Nintendo Switch 2, but the Android emulator scene has been busy marveling over something just a little bit different. One Redditor got the latest Zelda game running on Banana Native, a free emulator that’s available right now on GitHub.

Ocarina of Time remake performance has now reached a major milestone into the 30 FPS territory with frame gen, which means the game is actually running at around 15 FPS, but then scaled and interpolated using AI frame generation to appear like 30 FPS. Banana Native isn’t a traditional emulator, as it doesn’t emulate hardware. Rather, it uses the game’s own ARM64 code to run on the phone’s CPU. The Switch 2 is powered by a mobile Nvidia chip, after all.

The user is running the Ocarina of Time remake on a Poco F7 Ultra, which sports a two-generation-old Qualcomm Snapdragon 8 Elite (Gen 4) processor. The latest phones that are coming out, like the Redmagic 12 Pro, run a 6th-gen Snapdragon 8 Elite Extreme chip, so performance could be a lot better on a newer phone. Apps like Banana Native require specific driver support for each phone to run, and as I pointed out in my Redmagic 12 Pro review, platforms like Droid Deck and Game Native don’t yet have proper drivers for the newest chips.

9to5Google’s Damien Wilde recently reviewed Droid Deck, another native-type emulator that lets you legally play Steam games you own on your phone by just signing into your Steam account. It works extremely well on lots of Android phones and helps showcase just how far Android emulation has come this year.

Anthropic just launched its most powerful Claude ahead of its IPO — here's why it's telling users to stick with Opus

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Not everyone needs the company's most powerful models

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Anthropic has a new AI model, and it's more powerful than the Claude most people are using for chat. And that's precisely why the company doesn't actually recommend switching to it for everyday tasks.

Claude Fable 5.2, released Monday alongside the more restricted Mythos 5.2, is designed for some of the toughest work AI can handle, including advanced coding, scientific research and complex engineering projects.

The timing is significant. Anthropic is also preparing for a massive initial public offering, putting fresh attention on how the company plans to turn increasingly powerful AI into a profitable business.

For Claude users, though, the immediate question is simpler: what can Fable 5.2 actually do, and is it worth using instead of Opus?

Fable 5.2 is designed for projects that require more than generating an email, summarizing a document or answering a question.

Anthropic says its newest model can handle complicated technical work that would otherwise require extensive human effort. During prerelease testing, its models helped engineers complete difficult software migrations, design working electronic devices and tackle advanced physics problems.

One of the more interesting applications is using Fable as an AI advisor. Rather than asking Fable to handle every task, developers can have smaller Claude models consult it when they encounter a particularly difficult problem.

Sign up to the Tom's AI Guide weekly newsletter summing up all the biggest AI news you need to know. Plus, analysis from our AI editors and tips on how to use the latest AI tools!

Fable can also act as an orchestrator, coordinating smaller models and assigning them simpler tasks while it handles the more complicated work.

For everyday users, the most practical applications could include analyzing complex research, working through difficult technical problems or managing projects that involve multiple steps and large amounts of information.

Here's where Anthropic's lineup gets confusing. Despite introducing Fable 5.2 as one of its most capable models, Anthropic recommends that most people continue using Claude Opus 5.5 for everyday coding and knowledge work.

The company describes Opus as its strongest daily driver, while Fable is intended for more ambitious technical challenges. That means someone using Claude for writing, research, brainstorming or routine coding probably doesn't need to switch.

Fable becomes more interesting when a task requires sustained reasoning, complicated problem-solving or coordination between multiple AI systems.

There are also cheaper models in the lineup. Claude Sonnet 5.5 and Haiku 5.5 remain options for simpler work where speed and efficiency matter more than maximum capability.

The takeaway is that the most powerful Claude isn't automatically the best Claude for every job.

Anthropic has reduced the price of Fable compared with its previous version, but it remains more expensive than Opus.

Through the API, Fable 5.2 costs $8 per million input tokens and $40 per million output tokens. That's 20% less than Fable 5.1, although it's still twice the per-token price of Opus 5.5.

Anthropic also says the new model generates responses up to 30% faster than its predecessor. An optional fast mode offers even higher output speeds, but at twice the standard API token price.

For developers and businesses running large numbers of AI requests, those differences can add up quickly. If you run through your usage limits quickly, you might want to choose Opus 5.5 first.

For people using Claude through a subscription, access and usage limits also matter. Fable is available through supported Claude plans, although how much you can use depends on your subscription and any applicable usage credits.

Anthropic's latest model launch arrives at a particularly important moment for the company.

According to Reuters, Anthropic circulated IPO pricing information to banks before retracting it, saying the figures were inaccurate. The withdrawn proposal outlined a potential offering worth as much as $69.3 billion, although those numbers should not be treated as finalized.

The company is entering the public-market spotlight at a time when investors are scrutinizing the enormous cost of developing and operating advanced AI.

So, instead of offering one extremly powerful version of Claude for everyone, Anthropic is building a lineup designed for different types of work and different budgets.

Haiku and Sonnet can handle simpler, high-volume tasks. Opus serves as the everyday workhorse. Fable targets more demanding projects, while Mythos offers specialized capabilities to approved organizations working in fields such as cybersecurity and biomedical research.

It's a strategy that could help Anthropic serve more customers without requiring everyone to pay for its most expensive technology.

But it also creates a challenge: users need to understand why they should pay more for one model when another may already do everything they need.

As Anthropic moves toward its IPO, demonstrating that customers will pay for those additional capabilities could become just as important as achieving another impressive benchmark score.

If you're already using Claude Opus 5.5 for writing, research, brainstorming or everyday productivity, there's little reason to rush into Fable 5.2.

The new model is worth exploring if you're working on particularly difficult technical projects or tasks where existing AI models struggle to deliver reliable results.

For everyone else, Opus remains Anthropic's recommended starting point.

Why Is Everyone So Mad at Supreme’s Elliott Smith Collaboration?

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Earlier this week, Supreme announced an Elliott Smith capsule collection, including a selection of jackets and T-shirts featuring artwork from across the late musician’s career, from his self-titled 1995 album to his seminal 1997 album Either/Or to 1998’s XO and 2000’s Figure 8.

The internet seems to be in a huff over this, relying on a view of Supreme and streetwear frozen in 2008 that was, even then, embarrassingly myopic. The line of thinking goes that Supreme, the brand that was founded in New York in 1994 by designer James Jebbia and that has been a staple in skate and streetwear culture ever since, is an inauthentic purveyor of the ethos represented by Smith and his music. One might cite the brand’s roughly $500 million investment from the Carlyle Group in 2017 or its association with high fashion as disqualifying characteristics. More than anything, there seems to be a sense that taking shots at a nearly 40-year-old streetwear institution is a way to prove one’s unsullied dedication to a sense of anti-consumerism. The anger is misplaced.

In a culture awash in goofy collaborations and corporate cash grabs, Supreme might be one of the few brands still operating from an actual set of beliefs. Its Elliott Smith capsule (which was made with permission from Smith’s estate) is in a long line of collaborations that Supreme has done with musicians. Its first artist collaborator was Rammellzee, the experimental rapper and visual artist whose relationship with the brand dates to 1994, the year Supreme opened. By 2005, Raekwon was posing with a Tickle Me Elmo for the first of Supreme’s commissioned portrait T-shirts. Across its music collaborations, portrait tees, and tributes, the list includes everyone from Michael Jackson to the Velvet Underground, Nirvana, Butthole Surfers, My Bloody Valentine, Daniel Johnston, Aphex Twin, and SALEM.

There have been collections with record labels like Stax, Wackies, Rap-A-Lot, Ruff Ryders, Jah Life, and Duck Down, the last of which put Black Moon and Smif-N-Wessun’s album artwork on clothes. There are shirts and shoes with Peter Saville’s artwork for Joy Division and New Order. The Smith collaboration is so patently in the wheelhouse of what the brand has done for decades that bringing it up now as some sort of “gotcha!” feels more like a product of social media’s tilt towards rage-bait than any defense of an artist. Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century

Elliott Smith, who died in 2003 at 34, has long been a part of skate culture. He was friends with Mike Mills, the skateboarder and filmmaker who designed graphics for Stereo and one of Supreme’s early logos. Mills worked on unused cover art for Figure 8 and later enlisted Smith to record music for his film Thumbsucker. And Smith’s music has been celebrated in the world of skateboarding for many years now. Mark Baines skated to “Happiness” in Blueprint’s Waiting for the World in 2000. Nate Broussard used “A Distorted Reality Is Now a Necessity to Be Free” in Static III in 2007. Dylan Rieder skated to “Coast to Coast” in Alien Workshop’s Mind Field in 2009, and Mark Suciu used “Junk Bond Trader” in Cross Continental in 2012.

Arguing that he would have hated the collaboration, aside from being infantilizing, completely ignores the actual culture around Elliott Smith when he was alive, instead opting for some abstract representation of a person who actually existed in the real world — a particularly Gen Z problem, to be frank.

With these collaborations, estates and relevant parties are always compensated. And that actually matters in an era where an artist can remain a staple for young people for generations, long after they’ve died, while reaping diminishing returns thanks to the paltry payouts from streamers. Meaning, short of throwing him onstage as a hologram or doling out money-grabbing box sets, a tasteful collection with a brand with a real connection to his work is actually one of the better options that exists. Trending Stories Watch Ariana Grande, Ben Stiller, Robert De Niro Take Over ‘The Tonight Show’ Paul McCartney and Ringo Starr to Reunite at Las Vegas Show in 2027 Pink Becomes First Solo Female Pop Artist to Headline the Sphere Judge Sets Steven Tyler Sex Assault Trial Date

People who want to be mad at Supreme want to separate themselves from some sort of mainstream idea of cool. They want to believe that they have an authenticity that Supreme doesn’t. Sorry to say, the reverse is more likely true. Just last week, Business of Fashion published a report on the brand’s cultural durability. “Supreme isn’t cool anymore” is, in fact, a very uncool thing to say in 2026. And beyond that, the people inside the company are coming from an ethos that has remained steadfast in actually supporting youth culture.

To sit on the outside now and pretend that you’re such an authentic Elliott Smith fan that you’re upset by this is simply to expose how out of touch you are.
