The growing use of generative AI meant it was "harder to judge what students made themselves."

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Australian STEM Videogame Challenge, a "free national videogame development competition for students," has confirmed that the rise of generative AI means this year's event will be the last.

The end of the STEM (Science, Technology, Engineering, and Mathematics) Videogame Challenge was first reported on Reddit last week, when someone claiming to be a judge in the program said they'd received an email informing them that "after 12 wonderful years," the 2026 event would mark its end.

Speculation as to the cause of the program's cancellation focused primarily on the presence of AI in entries: Another self-proclaimed judge said the problem wasn't merely the use of AI in itself but that "some students just completely generate their games with AI. How do you mark that? You can't just give them a zero. It's so hard to tell what was or wasn't generated using AI, and especially hard since that's the way the industry is going."

That same person also suggested, however, that funding cutbacks could have been an even bigger factor in the decision, saying, "projects like this are the first to get cut" when government budgets get tight.

Now the Australian Council for Educational Research has confirmed that the cancellation was in fact due to AI.

"After more than a decade inspiring the next generation of young creators, the current 2026 Australian STEM Video Game Challenge will be the competition's final chapter," Cathy Zemaitis, Head of the Student Learning and Progress division at ACER, told PC Gamer.

"The decision was driven by the rapid and complex rise of generative AI in game development, making it harder to judge what students made themselves, and moving the program away from its original purpose."

Keep up to date with the most important stories and the best deals, as picked by the PC Gamer team.

So, that's that: A program aimed at encouraging kids to learn has come to an end because the kids learned how to get around the learning part. It's sad, although as a former high school reprobate myself I can't be too critical of the young'uns for doing what they do. I do wonder, though, what all of this might say about our future: Where will we be when we know how to prompt, but not how to create?

Zemaitis added that organizers are not looking at ways to adjusting the program to accommodate generative AI: "There are no plans to pursue alternative options for delivering the Australian STEM Videogame Challenge, however we're always looking for how we can partner with schools and the sector more broadly to deliver programs that enrich the learning experience of students." She also encouraged educators looking for other ways to "keep students challenged" to check out the International Mathematical Modelling Challenge.

Stanford’s 100-year AI study says scaling is running out of road

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Stanford AI100 report, published every five years, finds AI investment outpacing earlier booms and not yet paying off. It says the public has had little say in how AI is built.

Stanford’s One Hundred Year Study on Artificial Intelligence (AI100) published its third report on Tuesday. AI Goes Mainstream: The Scaling Era and the Rise of Generative AI sets out 12 takeaways on five years of AI. The last one is a warning about the Stanford AI100 report itself.

“This report will be out of date by the time you read it,” the panel writes.

AI100 started in 2014 and runs from the Stanford Institute for Human-Centered Artificial Intelligence. Every five years it asks a panel of experts to assess where AI stands. Michael Wooldridge, a professor at the University of Oxford, chaired this year’s 12-person panel. Its members include Peter Norvig, Toby Walsh and Sara Hooker.

“Since 2021, powerful general purpose AI tools have gone from being a point of speculation to an everyday reality, used by millions of people,” Wooldridge said in a statement.

Scaling means building AI with ever more compute and data. The report says it powered the main advances of the past five years. That approach is now running short of new, high-quality data, and compute for frontier models costs too much. The panel expects the next phase to rely on smarter algorithms, smaller models and more time to reason.

The money behind AI gets its own warning. Investment in data centres and chips has surpassed earlier booms and is straining energy and freshwater supplies, the panel writes. Nvidia alone accounted for more than 15% of US stock market gains in 2025, it says. Yet AI’s economic impact does not show up in macroeconomic data or in companies’ returns.

“AI is not yet paying its way,” the report says.

It also notes growing backlash against data centre construction in the US. Last week, US voters rejected AI data centres in 40 of 41 ballot measures.

Several takeaways deal with harm. AI systems have found serious security flaws in code, the report says. Teams of AI agents have also carried out cyberattacks. In war, militaries have made targeting decisions for lethal weapons without humans in the loop. The panel calls for new rules of engagement. Last month, Pope Leo urged a ban on lethal autonomous weapons.

The report says AI-generated images, audio and video have ended the assumption that recordings are trustworthy evidence. It lists AI agents breaking out of testing sandboxes as one risk that once seemed like science fiction. In September, OpenAI took 2.5 hours to stop an AI agent that escaped its sandbox.

AI is reshaping the lives of billions of people, the panel writes. Yet the public has had little say in how it is built and used. It also finds global governance fragmented and framed around national security, with little agreement between countries.

The panel also used AI to make the report. Its authors used AI tools to transcribe meetings, check drafts and compare it with earlier reports, its disclosure says. Humans wrote the text.

Is the Hope at AFM Real?

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Box office breakouts and a possible federal tax credit are lifting spirits among indie dealmakers — but disruption still reigns.

Film execs heading to L.A. for the American Film Market may be feeling something they haven’t in a while: hope.

Disruption still reigns, but independent and specialty fare has had a run of breakouts at the North American box office, topped by this weekend’s launch of Godzilla Minus Zero. The sequel from GKIDS and Toho opened to an estimated $32 million, well above the distributor’s mid-$20 millions projection. Takashi Yamazaki’s follow-up to the Oscar-winning Godzilla Minus One (2023) reportedly cost in the mid-teens and is being touted as the biggest live-action foreign-language opening since 2004’s Hero, which bowed with $17.8 million. Related Stories Movies Brandon Sklenar on 'Iron Jane,' Body Dysmorphia and His Rapid Hollywood Rise Movies Claes Bang, Valerie Pachner Drama 'I Is Another' Sells Wide Ahead of AFM

It isn’t the only surprise. A24’s You Can See Everything, the Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim documentary about Elizabeth Holmes, is entering blockbuster territory, having grossed almost $80 million domestically. That’s a rare result for nonfiction in a theatrical market that has been unkind to documentaries, and the film is only beginning to roll out internationally.

Niche programming is delivering, too. Angel Studios’ faith-based Drummer Boy, from directors Ben and Joel David Smallbone, opened to around $9 million. Nitesh Tiwari’s Indian epic Ramayana: Rise of a Legend, handled in the U.S. by Sony, grossed an estimated $8.6 million in its first weekend, on the widest domestic launch ever for an Indian film.

Add this year’s indie horror run, from Obsession ($500 million worldwide and counting) to Backrooms ($400 million-plus) and the self-distributed Iron Lung ($50 million-plus), and the makings of a good-vibes AFM are in place.

For sellers, the biggest takeaway is that audiences are showing up, and that they are young. Sarah Lebutsch, head of sales at U.K. group Protagonist Pictures, points to You Can See Everything as proof that a smart campaign and the right timing can still produce a hit. “Look at what You Can See Everything is doing at the moment in the U.S. It’s incredible,” she says.

She is equally encouraged by who is buying tickets. “It’s great to see those sorts of box office results, and the fact that Gen Z are going, and Gen Alpha are going and seeing films together in cinemas,” Lebutsch says. “There is a big resurgence of that, which I think we should all be very, very encouraged by.”

Policy may help, too. California Gov. Gavin Newsom is pushing a major expansion of the state’s film and TV tax credit program, proposing to raise the annual credit from $750 million to $1 billion. The incentive has been crucial for keeping projects in the state. Newsom is also backing the bipartisan effort in Congress, championed by President Trump, to create a national film and TV tax incentive that would stack with each state’s own credits. For indie producers piecing together financing, having a federal layer on top of state incentives would be a significant and much-needed change.

The presale market, long AFM’s foundation, also appears to be holding up. “At Protagonist, we’ve always done very well out of the AFM,” Lebutsch says. “Presales have always worked really well for us there. The big buyers from across the world, they’ll all be there to pick up packages.” Protagonist will be shopping It’s All Right, with Paul Bettany as an aging rock star and Asha Banks as a rising singer who, unbeknownst to her, is his forgotten daughter from a one-night stand.

The online creator-to-director pipeline will be a major talking point at this year’s AFM. Seemingly all the hot young horror directors honed their craft, and built their respective fan bases, online before their theatrical breakouts.

“The film industry can certainly learn from creators that storytelling doesn’t need to be limited necessarily to strict forms,” says veteran horror producer Steven Schneider (Paranormal Activity, Insidious), whose Spooky Pictures recently scored a hit with Hokum, which has grossed $25 million worldwide on a $5 million budget. “Stories can begin online, narratives can be told, shared and expanded through shortform videos and parsed out over time in a way that builds awareness and excitement. Creators have shown incredible prowess in meeting their audiences where they’re at.”

But Schneider cautions that success online does not always translate to cinemas. “Traditional storytelling conventions and techniques are established for good reason,” he says. “We want to innovate and bridge the gap between an online audience and a theatrical one, but it’s important to respect the differences in the form.”

Traditional filmmaking, he argues, can “connect more deeply and profoundly with an audience through intricate storytelling,” and that craft “shouldn’t be dismissed in favor of virality or easy dopamine hits.”

Not every indie package at AFM will become the next Obsession or You Can See Everything. But audiences are returning, U.S. tax incentives are on the horizon and a new generation of filmmakers is packing theaters with younger fans. For a market that for years felt like it was in terminal decline, that’s reason enough to cheer.

Xfinity offers free GTA 6 speed boost to download the massive 212GB game in 30 minutes

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Xfinity handed its internet customers a free 15-day speed boost of up to 2 Gbps, so they could download GTA 6 faster.

The offer landed just as Rockstar flung open digital pre-loads on November 10, confirming the hotly anticipated game would need up to 212GB, or roughly a third of a base PS5’s usable storage.

With a download that size looming, Spectrum had already promised its customers a free gig upgrade, and it didn’t take long for Xfinity to follow.

Comcast’s November 9 announcement confirms eligible Xfinity Internet members can activate the boost through the Xfinity app or the Membership page at Xfinity.com/membership.

Once claimed, it lifts download speeds to up to 2 gigabits per second for 15 days, which Comcast says is enough to download GTA 6 in as little as 30 minutes.

The redemption window stays open until December 3, so anyone who claims it on the final day keeps the faster speeds until December 18. Meanwhile, members who don’t see the option on their Membership page can contact Xfinity Customer Service to request access.

Comcast pitched the boost as a way to beat the rush. Kohposh Kuda, Senior Vice President of Consumer Product Marketing, said millions of people would “hit download the moment Grand Theft Auto VI becomes available.” In fact, Xfinity expected the stretch from November 10 to 19 to be the busiest week ever on its network, with players barreling toward pre-loads well before launch.

The company also pointed members toward its year-round Boost a Device feature, which prioritizes the WiFi connection for up to three devices, such as a console, on a home network.

GTA 6 fans have already waited long enough, after Rockstar delayed the game again to November 19 last year to give it extra polish. This time, at least, the download doesn’t have to drag on too.

DLSS 5 has been out for a while now, love it or hate it, but obviously not for AMD cards. Well, not officially, anyway. We have previously seen and tried mods that get the machine learning-aided neural rendering tech up and running using AMD RX 9000-series silicon, and now it looks like there's a significantly improved one.

AMDLSS5 version 2.0 is now out (via Wccftech), and it's claiming some impressive improvements compared to other DLSS 5 mods. Specifically, compared with "other mods", it's claiming +64% fps in Resident Evil Requiem, and +11% fps compared with the previous version of this one.

That's particularly great, if true, because our Dave's testing of DLSS 5 on an AMD Radeon RX 9070 XT using a different mod was, erm, not pretty. We're talking just 9 fps in Cyberpunk 2077, admittedly at 4K res on RT Ultra settings, but that was with FSR 4 on and set to Balanced.

It's not all the mod's fault, though, as DLSS 5 itself can be very intensive to run, even on an Nvidia GPU. Our Andy found this out when he tried running it in NBA 2K27. Frame rates there dropped up to 73% at 4K while using an RTX 5080, and there were significant drops at lower resolutions, too.

It's worth noting that some of the Reddit comments say the quality isn't very good with the mod enabled. That's to be expected, I suppose, for something unofficial, but it's just something to keep in mind if you're planning on installing it.

Installation itself involves simply downloading the files and running the installer. It then "installs, updates or removes the mod with one click."

GTA 6 is the ‘most pre-ordered game in PlayStation history’ despite the fact it’s locked at 30FPS, even on PS5 Pro

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Even a choppy frame rate can't stop the hype train.

It’s official, folks; Grand Theft Auto VI is now the most pre-ordered game of all time, and players who helped set that record can now start pre-loading it.

With over seven million pre-purchases and counting, GTA VI has been on track to become the most pre-ordered video game ever made for months. This morning, the official PlayStation account on X confirmed that GTA VI has finally reached that impressive milestone, formally dethroning Cyberpunk 2077, which held the title of “most pre-ordered game of all time” since its 2020 release.

Grand Theft Auto VI is the most pre-ordered game in PlayStation Store history!Mark the occasion with a video showcasing why GTAVI plays best on PS5Get ready for launch on November 19 by clearing PS5 storage space and pre-loading today: https://t.co/wTPa5E4DJY pic.twitter.com/WTQvHQs9cI

In a truly happy twist of fate, this groundbreaking moment synced up almost perfectly with Rockstar Games announcing through their website that GTA VI is now up for pre-loading. Anyone who’s already purchased a digital copy can now start downloading it to their console or PC, so it’ll be ready to play as soon as it releases on November 19, 2026.

As great as this news is, it also comes with a caveat. In the same post where they confirmed players can pre-load the game, Rockstar also explained that GTA 6 is locked at 30fps, even on the PlayStation 5 Pro, to ensure roughly the same quality across platforms.

Grand Theft Auto VI has been fully developed from the ground up to provide the best possible experience for the current generation of consoles, targeting 30 frames per second while pushing both platforms to their absolute limits to bring the vast and rich world of Leonida to life — while maintaining the density and visual fidelity of all its environments and inhabitants.

While plenty of gamers will be upset that they won’t be able to experience Leonida and all its unhinged beauty at 60fps, Rockstar confirmed that PS5 Pro owners will get a pretty crisp cut of the game thanks to PlayStation Spectral Super Resolution and higher ray-tracing support. It’s a small victory, but it’s a victory nonetheless.

In other happy news, Xbox CEO Asha Shar announced that GTA VI will be available on Xbox Cloud Gaming on both the Xbox Series X|S and Xbox One as part of a newly unveiled “ten-year strategic partnership” with Take-Two

Today marks the first step in our new 10-year strategic partnership with Take-Two. GTA VI, already the most pre-ordered game ever on Microsoft Store, will be playable on XBOX One through cloud gaming when it launches on November 19. https://t.co/WTtRs6plZx

As sad as some people are about the hard 30fps limit, it’s clear that the GTA hype storm isn’t going anywhere. So if you’re one of the millions who’ve already downloaded GTA VI, now’s the perfect time to get it onto your system.

Bilt CEO Ankur Jain is betting AI and sports fandom can drive local spending—starting with free beers when the home team wins

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sports has never been bigger—team valuations are skyrocketing, fanbases are diehard, and the money in the industry is growing faster than it has before. Now Bilt, the online rewards platform, wants to make going to a sporting event about more than just what happens in the stadium.

The company is expanding what it calls a “neighborhood operating system,” allowing teams and local businesses to connect with fans through personalized rewards, loyalty programs, and AI according to a press release Tuesday.

The expansion is another step in Bilt’s effort to move beyond its origins as a rent-rewards platform and become an operating system for local commerce—under the proposed system, local restaurants and small businesses can use Bilt’s platform to pair with sports teams and drive local traffic through personalized rewards. The company has already partnered with multiple professional sports teams to make it happen—including the Phoenix Mercury, Phoenix Suns, Houston Comets, Houston Rockets, Houston Texans, Sacramento Kings, and Chicago Bears.

“We think of it as expanding game day into the neighborhood,” Bilt founder and CEO Ankur Jain told Fortune. Teams could offer fans restaurant reservations before games, transportation to stadiums and watch parties at nearby businesses—alongside exclusive discounts and other benefits, he said.

Bilt is also bringing sponsors into the equation. Jain described an arrangement with Constellation Brands, the company behind Corona, in which fans participating in a team’s loyalty program could receive a complimentary round of the beer at a local bar if their team wins. The sponsor would fund the offer directly through Bilt and the bar’s point-of-sale system without needing access to individual customers’ private information, Jain explained.

As pro sports have expanded past television screens in the modern day, they’ve become a source of community: fans congregating at sports bars, stadium tailgates, and camaraderie.

According to S&P Global Market Intelligence’s 2026 Consumer Insights survey in July, approximately 74% of Americans watch live sports. Of those fans, 53% of Americans watch the NFL or college football, 36% watch baseball or basketball, and 20% watch hockey.

And that large fanbase means larger commercial opportunities that can extend past ticket sales. Sports teams have created opportunities to monetize their audiences through merchandise, sponsorships, memberships, and experiences past game day.

The global sports market generated an estimated $463 billion in revenue in 2024 and is projected to reach $610 billion by 2028, according to a 2024 study from investment bank Houlihan Lokey. Sports-related travel alone generated $111.2 billion in direct spending in the U.S. in 2025 as 339 million people traveled to attend sporting events, according to the Sports Events and Tourism Association’s 2026 State of the Industry Report.

According to Deloitte’s 2026 Global Sports Industry Outlook, AI and changing ownership models converged with sports, media, and entertainment as major forces shaping the industry—which is “inclusive of leagues, teams, federations, mega-events, media partners, investors, and more,” according to the report. The firm highlighted the importance of sports venues as year-round commercial destinations.

“AI has outgrown its role as a trick play,” the report read. “For many sports organizations, it’s becoming a whole new game plan. Moving into 2026 and beyond, sports organizations looking to compete shouldn’t ignore AI.”

The rollout of the new platform also impacts small businesses, according to Bilt. Jain said AI is an integral part of making the system work, especially for small businesses that struggle to manage multiple software platforms.

Bilt’s “Neighborhood OS” is at the center of the new system that rewards users for visiting local businesses, an AI-powered platform helps local businesses attract customers, build loyalty, and grow without replacing their existing systems—“designed to unlock” a $6.6 trillion opportunity in “local commerce.” According to Bilt, approximately $6.6 trillion of annual U.S. retail and food-services spending occurs in person.

Bilt’s AI Agents have completed more than 2 million real-world actions across more than 29,000 businesses, with 91% completed without human intervention in the past 30 days, according to the company. In one example, Jain described a restaurant potentially using Bilt’s system to recognize returning customers, identify previous orders, provide special offers, and combine existing software into one app.

“To me, the way a lot of these technology companies are looking at AI, they’re all talking about digitizing everything and taking away jobs and living online,” Jain said. “But our belief is that the biggest beneficiaries of it will be small businesses who can now use AI to help them run their fragmented business.”

Joshua Hong is a News Fellow at Fortune covering data, AI, cybersecurity, energy, and retail.

Sundrop turns your endless baby photos into recaps, time-lapses and more — without AI

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

New parents take a lot of pictures of their children, but those photos often end up languishing in photo galleries, never to be seen again after the initial snap and share.

A new app, Sundrop, aims to make those pictures more useful by turning them into weekly recaps, highlights, time-lapses of the child growing up, and other cute scenes, like putting the child’s photo on a magazine cover or a rookie card.

While Google Photos and Apple Photos already provide extensive tools to organize and manage photos, Sundrop is taking aim at a particular niche: Parents with a new baby (usually their first!) who take an overwhelming number of photos and want to do more with those pictures.

The app was developed over the past few months by the same team behind the read-it-later app, Matter: Ben Springwater, Sky Lan, and Rob Mackenzie (all dads themselves).

Notably, Sundrop claims it is designed to be private, and processes all photos locally on the device it is installed on. To make this possible, Sundrop taps into Apple’s Vision framework and on-device Core ML (machine learning) models instead of uploading photos to a cloud server or some other hosted AI service.

Both of the child’s parents can use Sundrop together by linking their accounts, which allows the app to sync their shared photos via Apple’s iCloud service. This feature, the company says, means the pictures aren’t sent to any external servers it runs.

In addition, the team notes that no generative AI is used in the creation of its scenes or highlights.

This differentiates it from many photo editing apps on the market, including Google’s, which are increasingly tapping generative AI to create new versions of your photos.

To find highlights and recaps, Sundrop scans pictures in the parents’ respective Camera Rolls on their iPhones, skipping things like screenshots or duplicates. It then creates highlights or fun scenes, which it calls “drops.”

One of these is a recap of the week every Sunday, announced as a Lock Screen notification. Others can put two siblings side-by-side at the same age, generate a time-lapse series of photos or a video of a kid growing up, or cut out a photo of the child and place it into a layered template.

Parents can also manually edit the photos Sundrop uses to make its creations, or use the app to automatically organize the best photos into galleries with other similar shots.

The app costs $39.99 per year, and offers a week-long free trial. The annual plan also includes Family Sharing, so only one parent needs to purchase it.

These complicated business moves helped her network secure thousands of hard-to-get Nvidia GPUs.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang admitted to having an affair with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, who is also suspected of being a spy working for the Chinese Ministry of State Security (MSS). Now, an investigation by The Information has revealed a 'labyrinth' of companies centered around Shanghai Century Litong and connected to Singaporean firm Megaspeed, which has previously been accused of smuggling $2 billion worth of Nvidia chips. The report claims Alice Huang helped move 240,000 banned chips into the country, mostly H100 and H200 systems, and was instrumental in securing AI GPU imports to the tune of $7.5 billion.

Sources in the report link the two companies to Alice Huang via their personal knowledge and corporate registration records, while there have also been reports that some of the Nvidia AI chips Megaspeed bought went to Shanghai Century Litong’s data centers in China. But aside from these corporate ties, there were also numerous reports about Ms. Huang’s personal relationship with the Nvidia chief, and how it gave her the ability to procure chips that were otherwise hard to get elsewhere.

Ms. Huang allegedly boasted about her access to the Nvidia CEO publicly. People familiar with her said that she regularly uploaded photos with Jensen to her WeChat account and even shared other images with customers to establish her credibility. She even claimed that Jensen Huang would often celebrate her birthday with dinner and gifts, while an Nvidia China sales employee said that she was a familiar face at the company’s annual event for local Chinese partners.

When AI shipments hit delays or faced some trouble, Ms. Huang allegedly would step in to get them moving through the supply chain again. One other person who knows her said that she would often cite her close relationship with Nvidia and its suppliers as the reason why she could always get Nvidia AI chips. It’s estimated that $4.6 billion worth of AI chips have been imported through Megaspeed from 2023 to 2025, with another $2 billion routed through Speedmatrix. It was also reported that Shanghai Century Litong bought GPUs from other sources, with the assistance of Alice Huang if the orders faced an issue, with the total value of the GPUs acquired estimated to be around $7.5 billion. The report claims Ms. Huang helped move some 240,000 banned chips into China.

Alice Huang may have appeared in Jensen Huang’s circle much earlier than previously believed. One source told the publication that Kang Wei, Alice Huang’s husband, met Jensen Huang during the 2010s, when Nvidia was primarily a PC gaming GPU manufacturer. According to the report, Kang Wei was a private equity investor focusing on videogame companies back then. In contrast, Ms. Huang reportedly worked for two media companies in China, although some say that there were no records of her doing so. She eventually jumped into banking and finance, working at HSBC and then CITIC Securities, before finally entering the data center industry in 2023 with Megaspeed’s pivot and the founding of Shanghai Century Litong.

Shanghai Century Litong is a data center operator founded in June 2023 by a shareholder that was ultimately under the control of Huang Yu, the sister of Alice Huang, whose Chinese name is Huang Le. The company then provided compute capacity to AI tech giants in China, including Alibaba, Tencent, and other smaller labs. A few months after its founding, it received coveted Nvidia Preferred Cloud Partner status in October 2023, which is an important milestone as Nvidia requires anyone with this status to have demonstrated sufficient capability in handling and running Nvidia hardware as well as generating revenue at a certain level, although that threshold varies by region and partner type.

In exchange for this, partners get better access to Nvidia’s limited number of chips, which became crucial for Shanghai Century Litong as the China-specific A800 and H800 chips were banned for sale to China around the same period. Although the move immediately banned the sales of these GPUs from Nvidia, it did not affect the chip stocks that were already in the hands of distributors. This meant that Shanghai Century Litong had priority access to the limited supply of these AI GPUs as Nvidia’s preferred partner.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Megaspeed, on the other hand, was reportedly owned by a company called Huang Le Limited and led by Ms. Huang when it pivoted in 2023 and was subsequently sold to Singaporean entity Swiftdata soon after this. Megaspeed then set up a subsidiary called Speedmatrix, which has two offices in Johor, Malaysia, a couple of months after that. The Bureau of Industry and Security (BIS), the U.S. agency in charge of monitoring export control compliance, noted that the Nvidia hardware found in Speedmatrix data centers was still in its boxes when they inspected the sites, triggering a deeper investigation into the company. Still, it’s been alleged that Nvidia intervened in the BIS probe, with the company retaining its Nvidia partner status to this day.

Despite her public closeness with the Nvidia chief, she’s rather elusive when it comes to her personal information. Two other people said that she has always been vague about what she does at her companies, and that she’s careful when it comes to signing documents and leaving a paper trail that could easily be followed. Still, the Chinese embassy denies any links to Alice Huang, even as the FBI looks into the situation.

PlayStation wins "hall of shame" award from consumer rights group

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Choice, an Australian consumer rights organization, has named Sony Interactive Entertainment as one of its annual "hall of shame" award winners. Alongside a vocal group of gamers, Choice is calling out PlayStation for failing to give players ownership rights to digital games, a problem that will be much worse with the impending end of physical games on the platform.

PlayStation wins "the Shonky Award for asking us to pay to play, but not own," Choice says in its write-up. According to Sony Interactive Entertainment, the organization argues, "you don’t actually own the movies and games that you buy as a digital download instead of a disc. Instead, you’re buying a license to access the content via Sony’s various digital storefronts, such as the PlayStation Store."

In effect, PlayStation is offering nothing more than long-term rentals for digital games, since it can simply revoke those licenses at any time. That fear will be intimately familiar to players who've been following campaigns like Stop Killing Games, and it's not an arbitrary concern – after all, as Choice notes, Sony did just delist several hundred digital films, revoking digital purchases with no refunds as they did so.

"You can't protect your licensed media based on the current EULA, and there won’t be any alternatives once the discs go away," Choice concludes. "You'll own nothing."

That lack of ownership may be a feature, not a bug, of PlayStation's planned end of physical media. The company still makes significant money on physical releases of its biggest games, but analysts argue that it's more interested in "control" of the market. No discs means no used games, which means Sony can take a cut of every transaction and set prices – alongside third-party publishers – without worrying about competition from secondhand markets.

Choice's Shonky Awards have been ongoing since 2006, and have sporadically included gaming companies in the annual hall of shame. In 2023, it named and shamed the Xbox Mini Fridge for failing to actually cool anything while drawing more power than a full-sized fridge. In 2013, when EA's always-online SimCity reboot launched to global server issues, Choice called out a problem particular to Australian players: the support line charged $2.48 per minute, "making people pay to complain."

AI-assisted firms increase flaw disclosures by 120% with no detected repair slowdown, study finds

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A preprint found a roughly 120% relative increase in disclosures against a Chinese-firm comparison group, with no detectable slowdown in the repair channels studied.

Software-flaw disclosures at firms given early access to Anthropic's AI model Claude Mythos Preview rose by roughly 120% relative to the change at Chinese firms without access, a November 4 preprint finds. Measured repair times showed no detectable slowdown.

Thomas Mosk's study finds that AI-assisted bug hunting was associated with more public disclosures without a detectable slowdown in the repair channels studied. Its findings cover publicly disclosed vulnerabilities and specific repair channels, leaving undisclosed workloads and users' installation of patches unmeasured.

Anthropic announced Project Glasswing on April 7, giving selected firms access to the model. The main disclosure analysis compares 13 participants with 267 Chinese firms from October 2025 through September 2026. The Chinese firms could use other AI tools. Selection into Glasswing was not random, and national disclosure practices differ, limiting causal interpretation.

Alongside the 120% relative increase in disclosures, the paper estimates roughly 150% more high-impact disclosures. That category means an above-median impact component in the vulnerability severity score.

Earlier Epoch AI analysis, published July 2, documented a spike in high- and critical-severity disclosures after Mythos Preview. It also warned that public counts omit undisclosed discoveries and can reflect increased interest in bug hunting.

Mosk examines fixing capacity separately, comparing Glasswing vulnerabilities with other non-Chinese vulnerabilities in shorter samples. One analysis follows 658 flaws reported to vendors by the Zero Day Initiative through June 2026. Another covers 14,855 vulnerabilities published through July and tracked by Linux distributions including Debian, Ubuntu, Red Hat and SUSE.

Neither repair channel showed a statistically detectable slowdown. For disclosed flaws, the distributions' median time to a fix stayed at one day. The vendor estimates rule out a slowdown larger than about 13%, although smaller changes remain possible. The downstream estimates still permit an approximately 11 percentage-point decline in the share fixed within 90 days.

For crypto infrastructure, the practical distinction is between finding a flaw, releasing a fix and installing it. CryptoSlate reported on Oct. 29 that Bitcoin Core disclosed three AI-discovered flaws after validation and patch releases. Installation across nodes remains a separate question.

The exploitation results also require care. In a comparison with large non-Chinese firms ending in May, before Glasswing's June expansion, Mosk found no statistically significant change in exploited-vulnerability counts across one-, two- and three-month windows.

The share of disclosed flaws known to be exploited fell in some comparisons. A larger pool of disclosures can lower that share without reducing exploited-vulnerability counts, however. Mosk treats the probability decline as suggestive; the count estimates remain too imprecise to exclude moderate changes in either direction.

A lower exploited share can reflect more disclosures rather than fewer exploited vulnerabilities.

Nonrandom access and national disclosure differences limit causal inference. The paper reports no statistically significant count change, not exact equality. It observes known exploitation in early windows, not all attacks, undisclosed flaws or user patch installation.

Hypothetical illustration, not study counts: 10 then 22 disclosures and 2 known exploited in each pool. Arithmetic verified with a calculation tool: 2/10=20%; 2/22=9.0909%, rounded to 9.1%; pool growth is 120%. These invented counts illustrate dilution only. Reported disclosure and exploitation estimates use different samples and comparison groups.

The observed disclosure surge came without a detectable loss of fixing speed. Whether that translates into fewer attacks or safer software in use remains unresolved.

Ripple CEO: Big XRP Holdings Don’t Mean Control

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Home » Ripple (XRP) » Ripple CEO: Big XRP Holdings Don’t Mean Control

Security measures within XRP’s native ledger ensure no holder on the rich-list gets to take over the chain.

Ripple CEO Brad Garlinghouse used the company’s Swell 2026 event to address a long-running claim that Ripple Labs effectively controls the XRP Ledger simply because it holds a large amount of XRP. He said ownership of the token does not confer authority over network rules or validation.

The XRP Ledger currently has more than 150 validators. Of the 35 on the current recommended unique node list, only one is operated by Ripple, roughly 2.9 percent.

Network operators decide which validators they trust and can configure their own lists rather than relying solely on any single published set.

Unlike proof-of-stake systems, XRP holdings do not grant extra voting weight. Independent validators reach agreement on transactions. Protocol amendments require at least 80 percent validator support for two consecutive weeks before they activate.

BOOOM! $XRP Holders🚨🚨🚨 @bgarlinghouse CEO of Ripple debunked one of the biggest misconceptions about XRP ledger!For everyone still claiming Ripple controls the XRP Ledger because it owns billions of XRP, listen to what Brad said at Ripple Swell 2026.Owning XRP does not… https://t.co/zyDgmkPmIM pic.twitter.com/mhpDrqLnXG

Ripple can propose software changes and invest in the ecosystem, but it cannot unilaterally force upgrades.

Three amendments have been activated this year: Permissioned Domains on February 4, Permissioned DEX on February 18, and Batch transactions on October 9, 2026.

Notably, these tools are designed to support more controlled environments for institutional applications while still operating on the shared ledger.

Several highly established financial firms have moved forward with XRP-related products. Aviva Investors launched a tokenized share class of its Sterling Liquidity Fund. Franklin Templeton, DBS Bank, and Ripple have outlined plans involving tokenized fund shares and RLUSD.

Standard Chartered has also announced live institutional custody of RLUSD on the ledger, with additional collateral work planned alongside Ripple and LMAX Group.

These bullish developments do not depend on Ripple controlling the ledger. They rely on the existing consensus rules, low transaction costs, and the ability of independent parties to issue assets and settle on the network.

Even without control of validation, XRP retains specific functions on the ledger. Transaction fees, currently as low as 0.00001 XRP, are destroyed. Funded accounts require a base reserve.

The OG token can also serve as an intermediary asset for bridging different issued currencies when that path offers better execution.

Mr. Garlinghouse’s comments restate a technical distinction that has been part of XRPL design for years: large XRP holdings do not equal governance power.

BYD offices raided as JLR legal battle heats up over alleged Defender lookalike

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The raid took place at BYD’s offices in the Netherlands, where bailiffs acting for Jaguar Land Rover reportedly seized documents before a court case this week over its “copycat” Defender SUV, the Ti 7.

After BYD opened orders for the Ti 7 in Europe and the UK in late July, many (including us at Electrek) compared it to the iconic Land Rover Defender, with its boxy, rugged (yet still elegant) SUV design.

BYD even described it as having “an unapologetically boxy profile” that delivers “the imposing, chunky design that finds favour among SUV buyers, yet delivers a contemporary look.”

Apparently, JLR has also taken notice. According to a report from The Times, court bailiffs acting on behalf of the British luxury automaker raided BYD’s offices in the Netherlands and seized digital records.

The raid comes ahead of a preliminary court ruling on Wednesday, which will determine whether BYD’s Ti7 SUV infringes on the intellectual property of the Land Rover Defender.

JLR believes BYD stripped down the Defender to reverse engineer some of its technology. Before the raid, BYD claims it fully cooperated with JLR’s requests.

According to local reports, BYD’s lawyers called JLR’s raid a “fishing expedition” and claimed, “too many files were seized.”

JLR declined to comment, while BYD said it had no comment at this time as it’s in an ongoing legal case.

The Ti 7 is on sale starting at £47,995 ($64,000) in the UK, on the road. That’s over £20,000 ($26,500) less than the plug-in Defender 110, which starts at £71,840 ($95,500) in the UK.

It has a seven-seat arrangement, which BYD said was specifically designed for the European and UK markets.

As the first vehicle in the UK based on its DM-p (Dual Mode performance) powertrain, BYD said the Ti 7 “vastly exceeds the closest competition,” seemingly another jab at the Defender.

The setup uses three electric motors and a 1.5-liter turbocharged engine, delivering a combined 402 hp (408 PS), enabling it to accelerate from 0 to 62 mph in 4.8 seconds.

The engine primarily powers a 35.6 kWh BYD Blade Battery, which provides a pure-electric WLTP driving range of up to 74 miles. In the city, it has an electric range of up to 102 miles.

Available exclusively in the UK in the flagship Excellence AWD trim, BYD claims that the Ti 7 offers “a level of equipment that exceeds anything that its rivals can offer at this price point.”

With the preliminary ruling scheduled for Wednesday, we should learn more about the BYD and JLR legal battle soon. Check back for updates as this is an ongoing story.

Arctic Town Kills Dozens of Grizzlies Feeding on Dump

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

For years, the grizzlies near Inuvik in Canada’s western Arctic relied on a stable source of food — the town dump. Generations of bears grew up feeding there. In 2025, wildlife officials found that over 40 grizzlies regularly visited the dump.

In 2025, before the fence was built, trail cameras captured multiple grizzlies hanging around the dump, looking for food. Photo: Government of the Northwest Territories

It was too much. This year, the town built an electric fence around the site, hoping the bears would move on. They didn’t. Some started coming into town looking for food. Others just hung around the fence, searching for a way in.

“They would just do these laps around the dump, and they would just be emaciated, sickly,” said Toby Halle, a Renewable Resources officer based in Inuvik, told the CBC. “They don’t know what to do with themselves. And quite frankly, we don’t know what to do with them.”

Inuvik, population about 3,500, was the first community in that part of the Arctic to try to wean the bears off their dependence on dump food with such a fence, which cost about $700,000. The problem was, the bears had not learned to depend on any other source of food.

“We’ve probably had three to four generations of bears that have been using that landfill,” said local mayor Peter Clarkson. In the end, the town took a drastic solution — but one that was probably unavoidable in the circumstances: They shot 28 bears.

Tragic though this is, officials hope that within the next couple of years, the remaining bears in the area will move on and learn to eat more natural fare.

Many other Arctic towns still have open dumps where bears come to feed. Photo: Government of the Northwest Territories

Jerry Kobalenko is the editor of ExplorersWeb. One of Canada’s premier arctic travelers, he is the author of The Horizontal Everest and Arctic Eden, and has just finished a book about adventures in Labrador. In 2018, he was awarded the Polar Medal by the Governor General of Canada and in 2022, he received the Queen’s Platinum Jubilee Medal for services to exploration.

Everything to know about Amazons extra-long Black Friday sale: Dates, daily deal drops, and more

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Amazon has nailed down its holiday plans. Today, the retail giant announced that it will host Black Friday Week and Cyber Monday sales from Nov. 19 to Nov. 30 — that's 12 straight days of deals.

In a company blog post, Amazon said its holiday savings events will feature "millions of deals across popular categories and new daily themed deal drops," including "double drops" on Black Friday and Cyber Monday proper. Here's what you need to know before you start filling your cart.

Amazon's Black Friday Week sale will begin at 3:01 a.m. ET (12:01 a.m. PT) on Thursday, Nov. 19, a week before Thanksgiving Day.

This will be the fourth year in a row that Amazon starts its Black Friday sale at least a week early. (The actual holiday falls on Friday, Nov. 27 this year.)

Amazon is hardly the only major retailer that draws out its Black Friday programming. Best Buy, Target, and Walmart are also offering Black Friday deals throughout the month of November — a win for early birds who are worried about popular gifts selling out as the holiday shopping season revs up.

Amazon hasn't officially said when its 2026 Cyber Monday sale will start, but in the past, it's kicked off the day right after Black Friday. With that in mind, Mashable's Shopping team expects Amazon to make the shift into Cyber Monday mode on Saturday, Nov. 28 this year.

Yes, Amazon has already dropped a bunch of early Black Friday deals. Head to amazon.com/blackfriday and look for the offers with a red "Early Black Friday Deal" label. (If you'd rather not sift through them all yourself, check out our running list of Amazon's top early Black Friday deals so far.) The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

Now through Nov. 14, Amazon is supplementing these with "early Haul-iday deals" on Amazon Haul, its Shein-rivaling budget storefront. Shoppers will encounter $1 flash deals there all week, plus a selection of 11-cent deals on Nov. 10 and 11.

On each day of its Black Friday Week and Cyber Monday sales, Amazon will run themed daily deal drops called "Today’s Big Deals." They'll involve limited-time discounts of up to 60% on popular products from brands like Balsam Hill, HydroJug, and Theragun, and they'll go live once a day except on Black Friday and Cyber Monday proper. Shoppers will get two batches of Today's Big Deals on those days: once at 3 a.m. ET, then again at 11 a.m ET.

Beyond those drops, here are the kinds of Black Friday deals shoppers can expect to see on Amazon in the coming weeks, it said:

Save up to 35% on Amazon's latest gadgets, including the new Alexa Photo Frame, Alexa tablets, Echo Show 16, Echo Time, and Fire TV Stick 4K

Save up to 45% on Amazon's 2024 Kindle Colorsoft, 55-inch Ember Artline TV, and 55-inch Ember Mini LED-Series TV

Get a $1,500 gift card when you buy or lease a new car through Amazon Autos from Nov. 12 to 30

Get three months of Amazon Music Unlimited for free (a $38.97 value)

Get three months of Kindle+ for free (a $35.97 value)

Save up to $600 on select Sony and Hisense TVs

Save up to 50% on select headphones and speakers from Beats, JBL, Marshall, and Sonos

Save up to 30% on select laptops and gaming PCs from Alienware, Acer, Asus, HP, and Lenvoo

Save up to 40% on select Ninja appliances, including the Creami, Foodi, and Slushi

Save up to 40% on select iRobot and Bissell floorcare

Save up to 30% on select Yeti and Solo Stove gear

Save over $200 on select Breville espresso machines

Save up to $80 on select Levoit air purifiers

Save up to $90 on select beauty tools from Braun and ghd

Save up to 50% on select Crayola and Melissa & Doug crafts

Save up to 45% on select Magna-Tiles, Mattel, Nerf, and Play-Doh toys

Save up to 30% on select Barbie and Hot Wheels toys

Save up to 30% on select Ravensburger games

Save 80% on Game Changer by Rachel Reid (the first book in the Heated Rivalry series)

Save 50% on Yesteryear by Caro Claire Burke

Save up to $500 on select Balsam Hill and Fraser Hill Farm artificial Christmas trees

Save up to 30% on select Hallmark cards, gift wrap, and ornaments

These will likely be some of Amazon's most competitive deals of the year. However, know that its prices may not be as low as they've been on Black Fridays past. That's because myriad products have seen price increases in 2026 due to factors like higher fuel costs, tariffs, and the ongoing memory shortage, aka "RAMageddon."

The latter is the reason why Amazon raised the prices of its Kindle e-readers, Fire TV Sticks, and most Echo smart speakers in August.

Amazon's Black Friday sale won't be exclusive to members of its Prime program. This is one of the main things that separates it from other Amazon events like Prime Day and Prime Big Deal Days.

No, Amazon does not price-match competitors' deals — not on Black Friday, nor any other day of the year. If you find a better price on something you've recently purchased there, you'll have to return it to Amazon and rebuy it where it's cheaper.

Most items purchased on Amazon from Nov. 1 to Dec. 31 can be returned through Jan. 31, 2026, except for Apple products, which have a return deadline of Jan. 15, 2027.

Haley Henschel is a Chicago-based Senior Shopping Reporter at Mashable who reviews and finds deals on popular tech, from laptops to gaming consoles and VPNs. She has years of experience covering shopping holidays and can tell you what’s actually worth buying on Black Friday and Amazon Prime Day. Her work has also explored the driving forces behind digital trends within the shopping sphere, from dupes to 12-foot skeletons.
